# Treasure-hunt routes for Spain’s main tourist cities

Research for City Quest: two candidate walks per city, a **classic** (the sights a first-time visitor must see) and a **local** (a characterful neighbourhood tourists miss), in the style of the Madrid Sol and Usera routes.

**Everything here is a draft.** Facts carry source links, but most were gathered from search summaries and Wikipedia; coordinates come from Wikipedia or OpenStreetMap (Nominatim); walking distances are straight-line legs × 1.3. Every route must be walked before it ships.

Each question is marked **sourced** (a source describes the visible detail), **likely** (well known, not found in writing) or **check** (unsure).

## Barcelona

**Visitors:** No official city-only figure retrieved. Catalonia (region) received a record 19,939,895 international tourists in 2024 (INE data, reported by Catalan News). A secondary statistics site estimates Barcelona city at ~14.5 million tourists in 2024 (~12.0 million international); treat as unverified. *Official city figures are in the Observatori del Turisme a Barcelona 2024 report (https://ajuntament.barcelona.cat/turisme/sites/default/files/2025-02/2024_C%C3%A0psula%201%20IAOTB.pdf) but that host was not reachable from this environment.*

**Official walking routes:** [Barcelona Walking Tours – Gòtic (official guided tour, starts at the Plaça Sant Jaume tourist office)](https://www.barcelonaturisme.com/wv3/en/page/4105/guided-walking-tours.html); [Barcelona Turisme – Street sculptures themed route (includes Brossa's Barcino)](https://barcelonaturisme.com/wv3/en/page/1952/barcino-by-joan-brossa.html); [Barcelona Turisme – Lluís Domènech i Montaner route (includes Casa Fuster)](https://barcelonaturisme.com/wv3/en/page/1190/casa-fuster.html); [Ajuntament de Gràcia – 'Itineraris per Gràcia' (PDF series, e.g. no. 3 on the Virreina estate)](https://ajuntament.barcelona.cat/gracia/sites/default/files/itineraris_per_gracia_3_-_visio_poliedrica_de_lantiga_finca_de_la_virreina_1770-2020.pdf)

**Notes:** Extreme crowding on La Rambla, Boqueria and the Gothic Quarter, especially May–October and when cruise ships are in; pickpocketing is common on La Rambla. Summer is hot and humid (July–August). Barcelona is mostly flat near the sea but rises towards Gràcia (gentle uphill from Plaça de Catalunya). Big festivals: La Mercè (around 24 September, big crowds, castells in Plaça Sant Jaume), Festa Major de Gràcia (mid-August, decorated streets, very crowded; Gràcia route squares fill up), Sant Joan (23 June), Sant Jordi (23 April – the Generalitat sometimes opens on this day), Diada (11 September – Fossar de les Moreres is the focus of political events; avoid or embrace). Overtourism is a sensitive local issue (anti-tourism protests in 2024); keep groups small and quiet in residential Gràcia.

### The Rambla to Mammoth Route · classic

*From the city's front room down La Rambla, through Roman Barcino, to a prehistoric giant in the park.*

Plaça de Catalunya (Metro L1/L3, FGC, Rodalies: Catalunya) → Mammoth statue, Parc de la Ciutadella (Metro L1 Arc de Triomf or L4 Ciutadella-Vila Olímpica nearby) · 8 stops · ≈ 3.4 km · 2.5 h · **needs a fix**

**Why:** It strings together the must-sees a first-timer expects (Rambla, Boqueria, Plaça Reial, Gothic Quarter, El Born, Ciutadella) on one downhill-then-flat line with free, outdoor questions at every stop.

**Already offered:** Yes, heavily covered. Barcelona Turisme runs an official guided Gòtic walking tour (https://www.barcelonaturisme.com/wv3/en/page/4105/guided-walking-tours.html), Frommers publishes a self-guided Barri Gòtic walk (https://www.frommers.com/destinations/barcelona/walking-tours/walking-tour-1), and dozens of free walking tours cover La Rambla–Gòtic–Born (e.g. https://freewalkingtour.com/barcelona/barcelona-historic-centre-spain/). The treasure-hunt format and the mammoth finale are the differentiators.

**To fix:** a 121 m leg (geofences may overlap); 1 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Monument to Francesc Macià | The monument to President Francesc Macià looks like a staircase. What is unusual about it? | It is upside down | sourced |
| 2 | Font de Canaletes | How many water spouts does the cast-iron Canaletes fountain have? | 4 | sourced |
| 3 | Mercat de Sant Josep – La Boqueria (entrance arch) | The market's official name is written on the colourful entrance arch. Which saint is it named after? | Sant Josep | likely |
| 4 | Plaça Reial – Gaudí's lampposts | Gaudí's lampposts are topped by symbols of the god Mercury. Which pair is it? | A winged helmet and a caduceus with snakes | sourced |
| 5 | Palau de la Generalitat – Sant Jordi medallion | On the Gothic façade in Carrer del Bisbe, a round stone medallion shows Saint George (Sant Jordi). What creature is he fighting? | A dragon | sourced |
| 6 | Barcino visual poem by Joan Brossa | Joan Brossa's giant letters in Plaça Nova spell the city's Roman name. What word do they spell? | BARCINO | sourced |
| 7 | Fossar de les Moreres | The red memorial arch holds an eternal flame for defenders who died in a famous siege. Which year is remembered here? | 1714 | sourced |
| 8 | The Mammoth (Mamut) of Parc de la Ciutadella | Which prehistoric animal stands life-size in the park? | A woolly mammoth | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Monument to Francesc Macià** · Plaça de Catalunya, south-west side (top of La Rambla / Rambla de Catalunya side), 08002 Barcelona · 41.386252, 2.170102

- The monument is by sculptor Josep Maria Subirachs and was installed in 1991. ([source](https://www.enciclopedia.cat/gran-enciclopedia-catalana/placa-de-catalunya))
- It shows a bronze bust of Macià in front of an inverted stone staircase, usually read as Catalonia's unfinished history. ([source](https://barcelonalowdown.com/monument-francesc-macia/))
- Francesc Macià was President of the Generalitat de Catalunya in the 1930s; the Generalitat's heritage site lists the monument on its Subirachs route. ([source](https://patrimoni.gencat.cat/sites/default/files/hd-images-zip/descarregues/2024/rutasubirachs.pdf))
- Plaça de Catalunya is the hinge between the old city and the 19th-century Eixample grid, and the starting point of La Rambla. ([source](https://www.enciclopedia.cat/gran-enciclopedia-catalana/placa-de-catalunya))
- *Note:* Very busy transport hub; pigeons and crowds. Sources differ on the staircase material (concrete vs travertine), so don't ask about material. Start point: Catalunya metro/FGC/Rodalies.

**2. Font de Canaletes** · La Rambla 129 (top of La Rambla, near Carrer de la Canuda), 08002 Barcelona · 41.385161, 2.170176

- The present cast-iron fountain dates from 1892 and is attributed to architect Pere Falqués. ([source](https://oldbcn.com/en/tourism/landmarks/canaletes-fountain))
- It has four spouts and is topped by the Barcelona coat of arms and a four-armed street lamp. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Font_de_Canaletes))
- Legend: anyone who drinks from it will fall in love with Barcelona and always come back; a bronze plaque at its base tells the legend. ([source](https://www.amicsdelarambla.cat/en/turismo-detalle/plate-with-the-legend-of-the-font-de-canaletes))
- FC Barcelona fans traditionally gather here to celebrate victories. ([source](https://www.barcelonaturisme.com/wv3/en/page/1213/useful-tips.html))
- *Note:* Sources disagree on the fountain's height (about 2 m vs 2.5 m) and do not quote the plaque's exact wording, so neither is used. Pickpocket hotspot.

**3. Mercat de Sant Josep – La Boqueria (entrance arch)** · La Rambla 91, 08001 Barcelona · 41.381493, 2.171394

- The market's official name is Mercat de Sant Josep – La Boqueria. ([source](https://ajuntament.barcelona.cat/mercats/en/mercat-de-sant-josep-boqueria))
- The modernista wrought-iron and stained-glass entrance dates from 1913–1914; the city council credits Antoni de Falguera. ([source](https://ajuntament.barcelona.cat/mercats/en/mercat-de-sant-josep-boqueria))
- The market stands on the site of the former Carmelite convent of Sant Josep, which gave it its name. ([source](https://www.barcelonabusturistic.cat/en/la-boqueria-market))
- Lonely Planet also attributes the gate and metal roof to Antoni de Falguera. ([source](https://www.lonelyplanet.com/spain/barcelona/la-rambla-and-barri-gotic/attractions/mercat-de-la-boqueria/a/poi-sig/1260157/1320690))
- *Note:* Answer is the market's official name; that 'Sant Josep' is legible on the arch itself must be confirmed on the walk. Architect attribution varies across sources (Falguera vs Mas i Vila vs Bergue) – kept to the city council's version. Extremely crowded late morning; question is answerable from La Rambla without entering. Closed Sundays (arch still visible).

**4. Plaça Reial – Gaudí's lampposts** · Plaça Reial, 08002 Barcelona (entrance from La Rambla via Passatge Bacardí or Carrer de Colom) · 41.380099, 2.175261

- The two six-armed lampposts were among Antoni Gaudí's first public commissions, around 1878–1879. ([source](https://en.fundacionantoniogaudi.org/?p=2330))
- Each is crowned by a winged helmet and a caduceus with snakes, emblems of Mercury, god of commerce. ([source](https://oldbcn.com/en/tourism/public-art/farolas-de-la-placa-reial))
- The central fountain is the Fountain of the Three Graces. ([source](https://aviewoncities.com/barcelona/placa-reial))
- Only two six-armed lamps were installed here; Gaudí's other lamps of the series went to Pla de Palau. ([source](https://en.fundacionantoniogaudi.org/?p=2330))
- *Note:* Dates vary (1878 vs 1879) and the Three Graces fountain's origin is disputed (Rovira i Trias vs made in France, 1876 vs 1878), so they are kept out of the question. Lively at night; mornings are calmer. Backup question: how many Graces on the fountain (3).

**5. Palau de la Generalitat – Sant Jordi medallion** · Carrer del Bisbe, corner with Plaça de Sant Jaume, 08002 Barcelona · 41.383434, 2.176202

- The Gothic medallion of Sant Jordi on the Carrer del Bisbe entrance was carved by Pere Joan in 1418. ([source](https://www.enciclopedia.cat/gran-enciclopedia-catalana/palau-de-la-generalitat-de-catalunya))
- It is considered the oldest sculptural representation of Saint George in the Crown of Aragon. ([source](https://en.ara.cat/culture/10-iconic-dragons-of-catalan-gothic_1_5714234.html))
- Marc Safont turned earlier buildings into a Gothic complex between 1410 and 1425. ([source](https://www.enciclopedia.cat/gran-enciclopedia-catalana/palau-de-la-generalitat-de-catalunya))
- Pere Joan's knight-and-dragon scene is the oldest depiction of it on the building. ([source](https://patrimoni.gencat.cat/en/node/17104))
- *Note:* Ara dates the relief 1418–1419; others say 1418. Police presence around the government building; don't block the door. The neo-Gothic bridge over Carrer del Bisbe is nearby (backup photo spot). Plaça Sant Jaume can be closed for demonstrations or events.

**6. Barcino visual poem by Joan Brossa** · Plaça Nova, beside the Roman wall and towers, 08002 Barcelona · 41.384167, 2.175118

- The seven giant letters spell BARCINO, the Roman name of Barcelona, next to the remains of the Roman wall. ([source](https://barcelonaturisme.com/wv3/en/page/1952/barcino-by-joan-brossa.html))
- Six letters are bronze and one, the N, is aluminium. ([source](https://oldbcn.com/en/tourism/public-art/barcino))
- The work was inaugurated on 24 April 1994. ([source](https://thisisbarcelona.com/arts-and-design/visual-arts/monuments-and-sculptures/barcino-joan-brossa))
- Plaça Nova faces the Roman gateway towers; the cathedral is just behind. ([source](https://www.oldbcn.com/en/ciutat-vella/nomenclature/nova))
- *Note:* Sources differ on whether Brossa created it in 1992 or 1994, and on Brossa's death year (1998 vs 1999) – not used. Cathedral (ticketed at times) is right here; the question needs no entry. Weekend antiques market and Thursday market on Pla de la Seu can partly hide letters – check.

**7. Fossar de les Moreres** · Plaça del Fossar de les Moreres, beside Santa Maria del Mar, 08003 Barcelona · 41.383684, 2.182433

- The square was the graveyard of Santa Maria del Mar; defenders killed in the siege of Barcelona, ending 11 September 1714, were buried here. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossar_de_les_Moreres))
- A poem by Frederic Soler says that in the Fossar de les Moreres 'no s'hi enterra cap traïdor' – no traitor is buried here. ([source](https://tramits.gencat.cat/en/diada-nacional-de-catalunya/historia/escenaris/))
- Anecdote: a gravedigger refused to bury enemy soldiers, even when one turned out to be his own son. ([source](https://fodors.fodors.com/world/europe/spain/barcelona/things-to-do/sights/reviews/fossar-de-les-moreres-164229))
- The square is the focus of events every 11 September, Catalonia's national day (the Diada). ([source](https://tramits.gencat.cat/en/diada-nacional-de-catalunya/historia/escenaris/))
- *Note:* Sources disagree on when the steel arch with the eternal flame was added (2001 per Barcelona Turisme/Generalitat vs 2002 per Time Out/Fodor's) – omitted from facts. Politically meaningful site; keep lesson tone respectful. Santa Maria del Mar next door (entry may be paid) is a great photo stop.

**8. The Mammoth (Mamut) of Parc de la Ciutadella** · Parc de la Ciutadella, near the lake / Passeig de Picasso side, 08003 Barcelona · 41.388416, 2.186255

- The concrete mammoth by Miquel Dalmau was inaugurated on 12 December 1907. ([source](https://oldbcn.com/en/tourism/public-art/mamut))
- It was meant to be the first of twelve life-size prehistoric animals that once lived in Catalonia; only the mammoth was made. ([source](https://barcelonanavigator.com/parc-de-la-ciutadella/))
- Parc de la Ciutadella was designed by Josep Fontserè, opened in 1881 and hosted the 1888 Universal Exhibition. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_de_la_Ciudadela))
- The park was laid out on the site of the hated 18th-century Ciutadella fortress, hence its name. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_de_la_Ciudadela))
- *Note:* Sources give conflicting dimensions (5.6 m high vs 3.5 m) – don't ask about size. Park is free and open daily (usually closes at night). Kids love climbing near it; it is fenced at times. The Cascada fountain and Arc de Triomf are nearby bonus sights.

</details>

### The Gràcia Squares Route · local

*A former village of squares, doves and Gaudí's first house – where Barcelona goes out for a vermouth.*

Casa Vicens, Carrer de les Carolines (Metro L3 Fontana or Lesseps, ~5 min walk) → Casa Fuster, Passeig de Gràcia 132 (Metro L3/L5 Diagonal, FGC Gràcia) · 7 stops · ≈ 3.0 km · 2 h · **needs a fix**

**Why:** Gràcia was an independent town until 1897 and still feels like one: small squares, a literary heroine in bronze, Civil War shelters, modernista markets and Gaudí's very first house, all outside the main tourist crush.

**Already offered:** Partly. Barcelona Bus Turístic describes a 'Gràcia squares' stroll (https://www.barcelonabusturistic.cat/en/gracia-squares), the district publishes 'Itineraris per Gràcia' PDFs, and private/free walking tours cover Gràcia. No official game-style route found.

**To fix:** 2 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Casa Vicens | Gaudí based the flower tiles on the façade of his first house on a plant that grew on the site. Which flower is it? | French marigold | sourced |
| 2 | Plaça del Diamant – 'La Colometa' | The statue here is of 'Colometa', heroine of Mercè Rodoreda's novel. Which birds are with her? | Doves | sourced |
| 3 | Plaça de la Virreina | The church presiding over this square is dedicated to which saint (locals still call it 'Plaça de Sant ...')? | Sant Joan | likely |
| 4 | Plaça de la Vila de Gràcia – clock tower | How many sides does the plan of the clock tower in the middle of the square have? | 8 | sourced |
| 5 | Plaça de la Revolució de Setembre de 1868 | The full name on the street sign remembers a revolution. In which year did it happen? | 1868 | likely |
| 6 | Mercat de la Llibertat | Francesc Berenguer's wrought iron on the market façades repeats a flower motif that is also on Gràcia's coat of arms. Which one? | Fleur-de-lis (lily) | sourced |
| 7 | Casa Fuster | Casa Fuster's façade is white marble. What colour are its sturdy columns? | Pink | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Casa Vicens** · Carrer de les Carolines 20–26, 08012 Barcelona · 41.40348655, 2.15064326

- Casa Vicens was Antoni Gaudí's first major project, built 1883–1885 (designed 1878–1880). ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_Vicens))
- Gaudí designed the pressed tiles from the French marigolds growing in the grounds. ([source](https://www.casabatllo.es/en/antoni-gaudi/casa-vicens/))
- The cast-iron railings were modelled on fan-palm leaves. ([source](https://atlasobscura.com/places/casa-vicens))
- Its style is oriental, close to neo-Mudéjar. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_Vicens))
- *Note:* Interior is a paid museum; the façade and railings are fully visible from Carrer de les Carolines. Sources disagree on build end date (1885 vs 1889) and opening to public (2017 vs 2018) – used Wikipedia's 1883–1885 only. Narrow pavement; keep groups to one side.

**2. Plaça del Diamant – 'La Colometa'** · Plaça del Diamant, 08012 Barcelona · 41.40405556, 2.15583333

- The square gave its name to Mercè Rodoreda's novel 'La plaça del Diamant'; Xavier Medina-Campeny's statue shows the heroine Colometa with her doves. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Diamante))
- The square is named after a jeweller, councillor José Rosell, who bought the land in 1860 and named his properties after jewels. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Diamante))
- Works in 1992 uncovered a Civil War air-raid shelter under the square. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Diamante))
- The shelter's entrance structures carry fragments of Rodoreda's novel in blue and of real witness accounts of the 1936–39 bombings in red. ([source](https://www.behance.net/gallery/43563377/Nous-badalots-daccs-al-refugi-antiaeri-232-(Gracia)))
- *Note:* 'Colometa' means 'little dove' – a nice lesson hook. Backup question: what colour are the novel fragments on the shelter entrances (blue). The shelter itself is only open on guided visits. Sculpture date/material not sourced.

**3. Plaça de la Virreina** · Plaça de la Virreina, 08024 Barcelona · 41.404947, 2.157228

- The square is presided over by the Església de Sant Joan, rebuilt in the 1880s by Francesc Berenguer, Gaudí's collaborator. ([source](https://www.lonelyplanet.com/points-of-interest/placa-de-la-virreina/1634408))
- The church was burned during the Tragic Week of 1909 and Berenguer led its reconstruction. ([source](https://www.timeout.es/barcelona/es/lugares-de-interes/placa-de-la-virreina))
- The square takes its name from the old Palau de la Virreina; its only surviving trace are medallions with the viceroys on the church façade. ([source](https://beteve.cat/general/palau-virreina-gracia-historia/))
- Many locals still call it Plaça de Sant Joan, after the church built there in 1884. ([source](https://moostips.com/en/placa-de-la-virreina-2/))
- *Note:* Whether the saint's name is written on the façade must be checked on site; if not, switch to a medallion question (what do the medallions show – the viceroys). Sources differ on rebuilding dates (1878–1884 vs 1880s; final rebuild 1951). Pedestrianised, terraces, good rest stop.

**4. Plaça de la Vila de Gràcia – clock tower** · Plaça de la Vila de Gràcia, 08012 Barcelona · 41.400224, 2.157576

- The Torre del Rellotge is octagonal and almost 33 m tall, the tallest public tower in the district. ([source](https://www.thenewbarcelonapost.com/en/a-civil-bell-tower-in-gracia/))
- Its clock has four faces so the time can be read from all over the old town. ([source](https://www.thenewbarcelonapost.com/en/a-civil-bell-tower-in-gracia/))
- The square was called Plaça de Rius i Taulet (after a Barcelona mayor) until it was renamed in 2009 after a citizen petition. ([source](https://barcelona.cat/en/discoverbcn/pics/la-plaza-de-la-vila-de-gracia-99400387399))
- The town-hall building opposite was designed by Francesc Berenguer and completed in 1904. ([source](https://barcelona.cat/en/discoverbcn/pics/la-plaza-de-la-vila-de-gracia-99400387399))
- Gràcia was an independent municipality until it was absorbed by Barcelona in 1897. ([source](https://ryo.co/en/articles/spain/barcelona/walks-discover-barcelona-foot/))
- *Note:* Sources disagree on construction date (1862–1864 Rovira i Trias vs c.1870 per Campaners) and one gives 38 m height – both kept out of facts. Clock tower is the main landmark of Festa Major de Gràcia (August).

**5. Plaça de la Revolució de Setembre de 1868** · Plaça de la Revolució de Setembre de 1868, 08012 Barcelona · 41.402664, 2.158146

- The square commemorates the 1868 'Glorious' revolution that overthrew Queen Isabel II and opened a six-year democratic period. ([source](https://www.barcelona.cat/en/coneixbcn/pics/la-placa-de-la-revolucio_99400391061.html))
- Its traditional name was restored during Pasqual Maragall's term as mayor (1982–1997). ([source](https://barcelona.cat/metropolis/en/node/1486))
- A Civil War air-raid shelter lies beneath it; only about 5% of the original survives. ([source](https://beteve.cat/historia/refugi-antiaeri-placa-revolucio-obrira-12-agost-festa-major/))
- *Note:* The full name is documented (Lonely Planet lists it as Plaça de la Revolució de Setembre de 1868); the exact wording on the enamel sign should be checked. Home of a famous ice-cream parlour (don't use shop names).

**6. Mercat de la Llibertat** · Plaça de la Llibertat 27, 08012 Barcelona · 41.399862, 2.153485

- The modernista ironwork was designed by Francesc Berenguer, working with architect Miquel Pascual i Tintorer; he used fleur-de-lis motifs on both façades, a flower also on Gràcia's arms. ([source](https://beteve.cat/societat/reconeixement-dels-125-dhistoria-del-mercat-de-la-llibertat/))
- Fodor's describes snails surrounding Gràcia's coat of arms on the market. ([source](https://fodors.fodors.com/world/europe/spain/barcelona/things-to-do/sights/reviews/mercat-de-la-llibertat-165100))
- Lonely Planet says Berenguer covered the market in 1893 with generous wrought-iron whirls. ([source](https://www.lonelyplanet.com/spain/barcelona/gracia-and-park-guell/attractions/mercat-de-la-llibertat/a/poi-sig/1204574/1320687))
- *Note:* Opening date disputed (1888 per city council vs 1893 completion) – the 'covered in 1893' fact is attributed to Lonely Planet only. Closed Sundays; façade visible anyway. The 'snails' detail is a good backup question but needs on-site check.

**7. Casa Fuster** · Passeig de Gràcia 132, 08008 Barcelona (at Jardinets de Gràcia) · 41.398001, 2.157866

- Casa Fuster was designed by Lluís Domènech i Montaner, with his son Pere Domènech i Roure, and built 1908–1911. ([source](https://barcelonaturisme.com/wv3/en/page/1184/casa-fuster.html))
- Barcelona Turisme describes its white marble and characteristic pink columns and trilobed windows. ([source](https://barcelonaturisme.com/wv3/en/page/1184/casa-fuster.html))
- It was commissioned by the family of lawyer Marià Fuster and has been a luxury hotel since 2004. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_Fuster))
- It stands where Passeig de Gràcia meets the old town of Gràcia, by the Jardinets. ([source](https://meet.barcelona.cat:443/en/discover-barcelona/districts/gracia/casa-fuster))
- *Note:* Completion year varies (1910 per Wikipedia vs 1911 per Barcelona Turisme). Hotel – answer from the street, don't enter the lobby with groups. End point next to Diagonal metro for onward travel.

</details>

## Bilbao

**Visitors:** No official full-year 2024 city total found. Jan-Nov 2023: 1,149,698 visitors, 50.9% from abroad. A Jan 2025 Deia article on municipal data gives a roughly 48% / 52% split between the two visitor groups (domestic vs foreign, as reported). Eustat says Bilbao hotel entries and overnight stays in 2024 were below 2023, while the Basque Country overall grew. Bizkaia province passed 2 million tourists for the first time in 2024. *The 2023 figure is attributed in search summaries to a Bizkaia/Ayuntamiento release; verify before publishing.*

**Official walking routes:** [Bilbao Turismo guided walk (90 min, from Plaza Circular office; flyer)](https://www.euskadi.eus/contenidos/evento/0000208590_m1_rec_turismo/eu_208590/adjuntos/plano.pdf); [Bilbao Bizkaia Card walking tours (Casco Viejo and Ensanche-Abandoibarra)](https://www.bilbaobizkaiacard.com/?p=710)

**Notes:** The official bilbaoturismo.net route pages could not be reached; confirm what's there. Climate: mild and rainy, so bring rain cover. Hills: the local route climbs the Calzadas de Mallona steps to Begoña. Big festivals: Aste Nagusia (Semana Grande) in August, Mallona fiestas by the steps (16-20 September 2026), Our Lady of Begoña feast on 11 October, BBK Live in July. The Casco Viejo floods with people on weekend evenings. Note: research hit a web-search limit, so some quiz details are 'likely'/'check'.

### The Puppy to Pintxos Route · classic

*A flower dog, a giant spider and a white bridge, then into the Seven Streets for arches and a market.*

Puppy, Guggenheim Museum Bilbao (Metro Moyua, tram Guggenheim) → Mercado de la Ribera (Metro/Euskotren Zazpikaleak-Casco Viejo) · 8 stops · ≈ 3.0 km · 2.0 h · **ready to write**

**Why:** It follows the Nervión from the Guggenheim's free outdoor sculptures to the Casco Viejo, linking new and old Bilbao in one flat riverside line.

**Already offered:** Self-guided route guides exist (https://www.nomadotravel.app/en/guides/bilbao-walking-tour), Questo has self-guided game walks in Bilbao (https://questoapp.com/es/self-guided-walking-tours/bilbao), Guruwalk free tours cover the Casco Viejo (https://www.guruwalk.com/es/visitas-guiadas/bilbao), and My City Hunt lists Zubizuri and the cathedral as hunt POIs (https://www.mycityhunt.co.uk/cities/bilbao-es-10226/poi/zubizuri-46760). Closest competitors: Questo and My City Hunt.

**To fix:** 1 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Puppy (Jeff Koons) | What is this 12-metre terrier covered in? | Living flowers | sourced |
| 2 | Maman (Louise Bourgeois) | Look under the giant spider's body. What is she carrying in her sac? | Eggs | sourced |
| 3 | Zubizuri | 'Zubizuri' is Basque for 'white bridge'. What colour is its arch and ribs? | White | sourced |
| 4 | Ayuntamiento de Bilbao and Oteiza's sculpture | In front of City Hall stands a steel sculpture by Jorge Oteiza. What shape is it? | Egg-shaped (ovoid) | sourced |
| 5 | Teatro Arriaga | This opera house is named after a Bilbao-born composer nicknamed 'the Spanish Mozart'. What name do you see on it? | Arriaga | likely |
| 6 | Plaza Nueva | Count the arches along one of the two LONG sides of the square. How many are there? | 18 | sourced |
| 7 | Catedral de Santiago | Find the Puerta del Ángel (the side door to the cloister). What pilgrim symbol is carved in its arch? | A scallop shell | sourced |
| 8 | Mercado de la Ribera | Look at the market's riverside façade. What are its big decorative windows made of? | Stained glass | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Puppy (Jeff Koons)** · In front of the Guggenheim Museum, Abandoibarra Etorbidea 2 · 43.26769, -2.934514

- Jeff Koons created Puppy in 1992, and it was installed for good at the Guggenheim Bilbao in October 1997, when the museum opened. ([source](https://worldcitytrail.com/2025/07/02/jeff-koons-puppy-in-bilbao/))
- Its steel structure is covered with about 38,000 flowering plants, watered by an internal irrigation system. ([source](https://worldcitytrail.com/2025/07/02/jeff-koons-puppy-in-bilbao/))
- Its floral coat is changed twice a year, in spring and in autumn. ([source](https://en.jardineriaon.com/puppy-bilbao.html))
- *Note:* Outdoors and free. Height is given as 12 m or 12.4 m - say 'about 12 m'. During replanting the dog can be bare or under scaffolding: have a backup question. Museum coordinates (Wikipedia): 43.26883, -2.934099; Puppy itself not geocoded.

**2. Maman (Louise Bourgeois)** · Riverside terrace behind the Guggenheim, by the Nervión (near Puente de La Salve) · 43.26916, -2.932953

- The museum's Maman is a bronze, stainless steel and marble work from 1999, cast in 2001. ([source](https://guggenheim-bilbao.eus/en/learn/schools/teachers-guides/maman))
- A sac of marble eggs hangs under her abdomen; the museum reads it as a symbol of a mother's ambiguity, protective yet trapping. ([source](https://guggenheim-bilbao.eus/en/guggenheim-365/perspectives/maman-by-louise-bourgeois))
- There are several Mamans: the original steel one at Tate Modern and bronze casts, one of which lives in Bilbao. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maman_(sculpture)))
- *Note:* Bonus: count her legs (8). Heights in sources range from about 9 to 10+ m - omitted. Leg from Puppy is about 150-250 m around the museum.

**3. Zubizuri** · Zubizuri footbridge, Campo Volantín / Paseo Uribitarte · 43.26638889, -2.92805556

- Zubizuri, also called the Campo Volantín bridge, is a tied-arch footbridge over the Nervión by Santiago Calatrava, completed in 1997. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zubizuri))
- It was designed in 1990 to link the Ensanche with Calle Campo de Volantín. ([source](https://calatrava.com/projects/campo-volantin-footbridge-bilbao.html))
- Replacing its cracked deck tiles costs the city thousands of euros every year. ([source](https://barcelo.com/guia-turismo/en/?p=8957))
- After the Isozaki Atea walkway was attached in 2006, a court awarded Calatrava 30,000 euros in 2009 because his design had been altered. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zubizuri))
- *Note:* Deck can be slippery in rain (anti-slip covering often reported but not confirmed by sources). Players cross the bridge to reach City Hall.

**4. Ayuntamiento de Bilbao and Oteiza's sculpture** · Plaza Ernesto Erkoreka 1 · 43.264014, -2.922978

- City Hall was designed by Joaquín Rucoba and completed in 1892. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilbao_City_Hall))
- It stands on the site of the former Convento de San Agustín, and artists of the day worked on its rich decoration. ([source](https://www.spain.info/es/lugares-interes/ayuntamiento-bilbao/))
- On the upper floor of the main façade Rucoba placed five oculi (round windows) with busts. ([source](https://ojs.ehu.eus/index.php/ausart/article/download/11955/11812/47734))
- Oteiza's 'Variante ovoide de la desocupación de la esfera' stands in front of the building. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilbao_City_Hall))
- *Note:* Alternative visual question: count the oculi with busts (5). Sources disagree on style (Baroque / Second Empire / eclectic) - omitted. Sculpture size (8 m) is from a single low-reliability guide - omitted.

**5. Teatro Arriaga** · Plaza Arriaga 1, Casco Viejo · 43.25948488, -2.92491985

- Opened on 31 May 1890 after four years of works led by architect Joaquín Rucoba. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Arriaga))
- Its design was modelled on the Paris Opera. ([source](https://www.spain.info/es/lugares-interes/teatro-arriaga/))
- It honours composer Juan Crisóstomo Arriaga, called 'the Spanish Mozart'; newspaper ads first used the name 'Teatro Arriaga' in 1902. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Arriaga))
- It was rebuilt in 1985 after severe flooding in August 1983. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Arriaga))
- *Note:* Confirm the name is visibly lettered on the façade, or swap for a count of something on it. A 1914 fire is claimed online but unverified - omitted.

**6. Plaza Nueva** · Plaza Nueva (Plaza Barria), Casco Viejo · 43.2591, -2.9227

- A rectangular arcaded square with 18 arches on two sides and 15 on the other two. ([source](https://turismo.euskadi.eus/es/plaza-nueva-bilbao/webtur00-content/es/))
- Neoclassical in style: designed in 1821 and inaugurated in 1851. ([source](https://www.spain.info/es/lugares-interes/plaza-nueva-bilbao/))
- In Basque it is called Plaza Barria. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Nueva,_Bilbao))
- *Note:* Whether corner arches count is unclear (the total could read 64 or 66) - test the count on the walk. Busy pintxo bars; Sunday morning market. Metro Casco Viejo exit is nearby.

**7. Catedral de Santiago** · Plaza de Santiago, Casco Viejo · 43.256944, -2.923889

- A scallop shell in the arch of the Puerta del Ángel reflects the church's place on the Camino de Santiago; the door is also called the Pilgrims' Door. ([source](https://es.hispanopedia.com/wiki/Catedral_de_Santiago_(Bilbao)))
- The door's name comes from an altarpiece to the archangel Saint Michael that once stood in the cloister. ([source](https://es-academic.com/dic.nsf/eswiki/238585))
- The south portico was begun in 1580 and took a century to finish. ([source](https://ninos.kiddle.co/Catedral_de_Santiago_(Bilbao)))
- The city's patron is Santiago el Mayor, and the cathedral lies on the coastal branch of the Camino. ([source](https://www.barcelo.com/guia-turismo/es/espana/bilbao/que-ver/catedral-de-santiago-de-bilbao/))
- *Note:* Consecration and cathedral dates conflict between sources (1643 / 1949 / 1955) - omitted. Fountain in the square (Carlos III era / 'fuente de Paret') could be a backup question.

**8. Mercado de la Ribera** · Calle de la Ribera (Erribera kalea), on the riverbank by Puente de San Antón · 43.25552222, -2.92424167

- The current market opened on 22 August 1929, a rationalist reinforced-concrete building that was very modern for its time. ([source](https://www.deia.eus/contando-historias/2020/09/06/tradicion-recuesta-ria-4682410.html))
- It was designed by architect Pedro de Ispizúa. ([source](https://www.mercasa.es/wp-content/uploads/2023/04/YDRAY-15-Mercasa_Mercado.pdf))
- With 10,000 m2 it is one of the largest covered markets in Europe, also known for its stained-glass windows. ([source](https://www.spain.info/es/lugares-interes/mercado-ribera/))
- In 1990 it earned a Guinness record as the most complete municipal food market. ([source](https://turismo.euskadi.eus/es/agenda/mercado-de-la-ribera/webtur00-evento/es/))
- *Note:* The Guinness criterion varies between sources (most complete vs most stalls) - phrase carefully. Market hours vary and it is closed on Sundays per guides; the question is on the exterior.

</details>

### The Amatxu Route · local

*Climb the old steps to Bilbao's beloved 'Amatxu', find a factory chimney, then drop over the oldest bridge into Bilbao La Vieja.*

Plaza Miguel de Unamuno (Metro Casco Viejo) → Plaza Saralegi, Bilbao La Vieja · 6 stops · ≈ 3.5 km · 2.25 h · **needs a fix**

**Why:** It shows the Bilbao of steps, faith, iron and immigration: Begoña above, the Etxebarria factory hill, and the creative, multicultural Bilbao La Vieja that tourists rarely cross into.

**Already offered:** No published tourism-office route combining Begoña, Etxebarria and Bilbao La Vieja was found; mural walks exist informally (e.g. https://wanderlog.com/place/details/7220627/ruta-de-los-murales-en-bilbao) and Bilbao La Vieja creative-space routes are listed by Planomato (https://www.planomato.com/es/bilbao/n/ruta-arte-vanguardia-espacios-creativos).

**To fix:** 3 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Plaza Miguel de Unamuno | The writer Miguel de Unamuno was born in Bilbao. What form does his monument in this square take? | A bronze bust | sourced |
| 2 | Basílica de Begoña (via the Calzadas de Mallona) | You've climbed the steps. How many bell towers does the basilica have? | 1 | likely |
| 3 | Parque Etxebarria chimney | What tall relic of the old Etxebarria factory still stands in the park? | A brick chimney | sourced |
| 4 | Iglesia y Puente de San Antón | This church and bridge appear on Bilbao's coat of arms. Which animals appear with them? | Two wolves | check |
| 5 | Plaza del Corazón de María | Archaeologists found the remains of what under this square? | A 16th-century Franciscan convent | check |
| 6 | Plaza Saralegi | What tall reminder of the neighbourhood's industrial and mining past stands in this square? | A chimney | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Plaza Miguel de Unamuno** · Plaza Miguel de Unamuno, Casco Viejo (by the Metro Casco Viejo exit and Euskal Museoa) · 43.25844444, -2.92154722

- Every 29 September the City Council lays flowers before Unamuno's bust in this Casco Viejo square. ([source](https://www.deia.eus/bizkaia/vida-social/2020/09/30/bilbao-grande-celebra-san-miguel/1068132.html))
- In 2026 that tribute moved inside the Euskal Museoa (Basque Museum) because of a labour protest. ([source](https://www.planomato.com/es/bilbao/n/bilbao-homenaje-unamuno-huella-cultural))
- The Calzadas de Mallona, a long stone staircase up to the Basílica de Begoña, start from this square. ([source](https://www.festivalesdeespana.com/festival/fiestas-de-mallona-bilbao))
- *Note:* Sculptor and date (Victorio Macho, 1984) come only from a low-reliability guide - omitted. Don't confuse it with the 2010 Unamuno bust by Luis Larrinaga inside Bidebarrieta library.

**2. Basílica de Begoña (via the Calzadas de Mallona)** · Top of the Calzadas de Mallona, Begoña · 43.25868611, -2.91384722

- Our Lady of Begoña is the patron of Bizkaia, called 'Amatxu' (little mother) by locals; her feast day is 11 October. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_Bego%C3%B1a))
- The Gothic basilica was begun in the early 16th century; one source gives 1511. ([source](https://www.spain.info/fr/curiosites/basilique-begona/))
- The current tower and part of the exterior were designed by José María Basterra and built between 1902 and 1907. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_Bego%C3%B1a))
- In 1873, in the Third Carlist War, the basilica was bombarded and its bell tower collapsed onto the nave. ([source](https://www.barcelo.com/guia-turismo/es/espana/bilbao/que-ver/basilica-de-nuestra-senora-de-begona/))
- *Note:* STEEP: the Calzadas de Mallona are a long stone staircase (step count not found in sources - a great counting question once walked). The public lift from Calle Esperanza is an alternative for buggies/wheelchairs (check it's running). Mallona fiestas on the steps 16-20 Sept 2026.

**3. Parque Etxebarria chimney** · Parque Etxebarria, Begoña hillside above the Casco Viejo · 43.26222222, -2.91944444

- The chimney was raised in 1943 and is 25 m tall, visible from much of Bilbao. ([source](https://www.barcelo.com/guia-turismo/es/espana/bilbao/que-ver/parque-etxebarria/))
- The factory began in 1878 when José Echevarría and his sons bought the Rekalde farmhouse and its land. ([source](https://www.barcelo.com/guia-turismo/es/espana/bilbao/que-ver/parque-etxebarria/))
- The park opened in 1990 after works that began in 1987; a 2023 remodel added a small lake and a wood. ([source](https://turismovasco.com/bizkaia/que-ver-bizkaia/parque-etxebarria-de-bilbao/))
- *Note:* 'Brick' is likely but not confirmed - check. Foundry closure date (1982) is single-source - omitted. Slopes; the long descent toward Atxuri/San Antón follows.

**4. Iglesia y Puente de San Antón** · Puente de San Antón / Calle Ribera, Atxuri · 43.255, -2.92333333

- Bilbao's coat of arms shows a two-arched bridge, the church of San Antón and two wolves above waves. ([source](https://www.heraldry-wiki.com/wiki/Bilbao))
- Fourteenth-century documents already record a solid stone bridge at this spot. ([source](https://ojs.ehu.eus/index.php/Bidebarrieta/article/view/20803/18680))
- The church was declared a national Historic-Artistic Monument on 17 July 1984. ([source](https://turismo.euskadi.eus/es/patrimonio-cultural/iglesia-de-san-anton/webtur00-content/es/))
- *Note:* Marked 'check': confirm the coat of arms is displayed somewhere visible here (lamp posts, plaque); otherwise change the question. Church and bridge dates conflict between sources (1468 vs 1510; modern bridge 1871-77 vs 1880) - omitted. Mercado de la Ribera (classic route) is next door.

**5. Plaza del Corazón de María** · Plaza del Corazón de María, San Francisco / Bilbao La Vieja · 43.256351, -2.926778

- The square shows the remains of a 16th-century Franciscan convent and a later military barracks. ([source](https://bizkaiatalent.eus/actividad-sociocultural-yacimiento-bilbao))
- The finds slowed the redevelopment of this pedestrian square, now the nerve centre of Bilbao La Vieja. ([source](https://www.bilbaoria2000.org/en/actions/bilbao-la-vieja/plaza-del-corazon-de-maria/))
- Bilbao La Vieja and the 'Allende la Puente' area lived off iron ore from the Miribilla mines. ([source](https://www.deia.eus/contando-historias/2024/01/01/vida-allende-puente-actual-barrio-7696222.html))
- *Note:* Check what part of the remains can be seen without entering (the site opens only on some days, e.g. free during the 16 May 2026 Rastro). Lively, multicultural area; fine by day, ordinary city-centre caution at night.

**6. Plaza Saralegi** · Plaza Saralegi, Bilbao La Vieja · 43.253788, -2.925217

- Traces of the mining past remain in Bilbao La Vieja, such as the chimney in Plaza Saralegi. ([source](https://www.deia.eus/contando-historias/2024/01/01/vida-allende-puente-actual-barrio-7696222.html))
- Mining was the economic base of the left bank here, with iron from Miribilla. ([source](https://www.deia.eus/contando-historias/2024/01/01/vida-allende-puente-actual-barrio-7696222.html))
- Bilbao La Vieja is now a creative hub of galleries and studios. ([source](https://www.planomato.com/es/bilbao/n/ruta-arte-vanguardia-espacios-creativos))
- *Note:* Exact position and leg distance from stop 5 not verified - check on the map. Questions repeat the chimney theme of stop 3; swap one for a permanent mural or plaque if you find a better one.

</details>

## Cádiz

**Visitors:** Not found. The session's web-search budget ran out before visitor statistics for Cádiz could be researched; to be filled from INE's hotel occupancy survey or the city tourism office.

**Official walking routes:** Not verified

**Notes:** The old town sits on a flat, almost-island peninsula: no hills, but long legs along the sea walls (Campo del Sur, Alameda) are exposed to sun and to the strong Levante wind. Carnival (February) takes over the whole old town, and the official carnival-group contest is held in the Gran Teatro Falla in January-February (es.wikipedia Carnaval de Cádiz). Expect very dense crowds and street performances in La Viña then. Summer weekends crowd La Caleta beach. Cathedral and Torre Tavira interiors need tickets; every question here uses exteriors only.

### The Pepa Route · classic

*Walk the city that wrote Spain's first constitution, from its monument to its sea-front park.*

Plaza de España (Monumento a la Constitución de 1812) → Parque Genovés · 8 stops · ≈ 3.1 km · 2.0 h · **ready to write**

**Why:** One flat loop-free line through the 1812 Constitution story, the Pópulo medieval gate, the golden-domed cathedral, the market, and the watchtower city of the America trade, ending in the sea-front park.

**Already offered:** Probably covered by city-centre free walking tours, but not verified: the web search budget ran out before competitors for Cádiz could be checked.

**To fix:** 1 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Monumento a la Constitución de 1812 (Monumento a las Cortes) | At the top of the monument, columns shaped like women (caryatids) hold up a giant stone version of what? | The 1812 Constitution | sourced |
| 2 | Arco del Pópulo (Plaza de San Juan de Dios) | Above the old town gate is a little chapel. How many staircases lead up to it, one on each side of the arch? | Two | likely |
| 3 | Catedral de Cádiz | Look up at the great dome. What colour are its tiles? | Gold / yellow | sourced |
| 4 | Mercado Central de Abastos | The market sits inside a square of classical columns. Which style are they? | Doric | sourced |
| 5 | Torre Tavira | The tallest watchtower in old Cádiz stands on a street corner. Read the street signs: which street meets Calle Marqués del Real Tesoro here? | Calle Sacramento | sourced |
| 6 | Oratorio de San Felipe Neri | The façade is covered with plaques honouring the 1812 deputies. Many were put up for a centenary. Which year? | 1912 | sourced |
| 7 | Plaza de Mina | A plaque on house No. 3 marks the birthplace of a famous Cádiz composer. Who? | Manuel de Falla | sourced |
| 8 | Parque Genovés | Find the fountain 'Niños bajo el paraguas'. What are the children sheltering under? | An umbrella | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Monumento a la Constitución de 1812 (Monumento a las Cortes)** · Plaza de España, 11006 Cádiz · 36.5351071, -6.29308746

- The monument was built in 1912 for the Constitution's centenary, designed by architect Modesto López Otero with sculptor Aniceto Marinas. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monumento_a_la_Constituci%C3%B3n_de_1812))
- It has allegories of War, Peace, Agriculture and Industry, and reliefs about the city's resistance during the Peninsular War. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monumento_a_la_Constituci%C3%B3n_de_1812))
- Female columns (caryatids) hold up the piece representing the 1812 constitutional code. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monumento_a_la_Constituci%C3%B3n_de_1812))
- After Ferdinand VII returned to absolutist rule in 1814, most of the constitution squares and monuments that sprang up across the Spanish Americas were destroyed. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monumento_a_la_Constituci%C3%B3n_de_1812))
- *Note:* The station is close by (Plaza de Sevilla), but check the walking distance. The square is a garden with a monument; at night the monument may be fenced.

**2. Arco del Pópulo (Plaza de San Juan de Dios)** · Arco del Pópulo, between Plaza de San Juan de Dios and Calle San Juan de Dios / Barrio del Pópulo · 36.529557, -6.293615

- In about 1587 mayor Mesa had a painting of the Virgin put above the medieval town's main gate to calm the quarrels of the crowds at the market there. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Capilla_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_del_P%C3%B3pulo))
- The painting carried the Latin prayer 'Ave María, Ora Pro Pópulo', and the gate took its name from it. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Capilla_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_del_P%C3%B3pulo))
- In 1596 the Earl of Essex's Anglo-Dutch troops sacked the town, and the painting was shot at several times. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Capilla_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_del_P%C3%B3pulo))
- The present chapel, begun in May 1621, sits on a large stone arch the size of the old gate. Two staircases, one on each side of the arch, lead up to it. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Capilla_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_del_P%C3%B3pulo))
- *Note:* Plaza de San Juan de Dios holds the town hall; no sourced details were gathered for it, so it is used only as the meeting point. Check the two staircases are visible from the street. El Pópulo is the medieval core, and its narrow lanes are lovely to walk.

**3. Catedral de Cádiz** · Plaza de la Catedral, 11005 Cádiz · 36.529, -6.2952

- Building began in 1722 to plans by Vicente Acero. The cathedral was declared finished on 28 Nov 1838, though the towers and other parts were still pending. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- The main dome is covered outside with golden tiles that make it glow by day. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- Its towers, finished in 1845 and 1862, are the city's highest points: about 54 m from the ground, 58 m above sea level. Tall bell towers were normally forbidden by the Bourbons because they made easy targets. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- Money ran short, so the materials are mixed: Genoese marble, jasper, limestone and the local 'piedra ostionera' (oyster stone) for the outer walls. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- In the crypt, below sea level, lie composer Manuel de Falla and writer José María Pemán. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- *Note:* The interior and the tower need a ticket. The seaward side (Campo del Sur) shows the dome best. The Roman theatre is next door and is used in the local route.

**4. Mercado Central de Abastos** · Plaza de la Libertad s/n, 11005 Cádiz · 36.53039641, -6.29823522

- The market was designed by Torcuato Benjumeda as a neoclassical square with a Doric portico, on the orchard of the Descalzos convent, and opened in 1838. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_abastos_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- Seville architect Juan Talavera y Heredia remodelled it from 11 Dec 1926, keeping the 4 m Doric columns. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_abastos_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- Working cisterns found where new foundations were due to go held up the works. The outside was finished on 1 Nov 1927 and the building at the end of 1929. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_abastos_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- Anecdote: Mayor Ramón de Carranza made the stallholders wear white clothes with sleeve covers, and the rule inspired a Carnival tango. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_abastos_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- *Note:* Free; busiest in the morning, closed Sundays. The fish hall is a highlight. There is a tapas area added in the 21st century.

**5. Torre Tavira** · Calle Marqués del Real Tesoro 10 (corner of Calle Sacramento), 11001 Cádiz · 36.532017, -6.2983833

- It is about 33 m above the ground and 45 m above sea level. Only the cathedral towers stand higher. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_Tavira))
- It became the port's official watchtower in 1778 and is named after its first watchman, frigate lieutenant Antonio Tavira. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_Tavira))
- More than a hundred merchants' watchtowers survive from Cádiz's 18th-century boom, after Philip V moved the Casa de Contratación from Seville to Cádiz in 1717. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_Tavira))
- Its camera obscura, open since 1994 and inspired by Edinburgh's, projects a live 360° view of the city onto a white dish for 15-20 people. More than 100,000 people visit each year. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_Tavira))
- *Note:* Going up costs money; the question uses the corner street signs. Check the house number. The leg to San Felipe Neri is short, about 160 m.

**6. Oratorio de San Felipe Neri** · Calle Santa Inés 38, 11003 Cádiz · 36.53295092, -6.29964689

- This baroque oval church was built in 1685-1719 by Blas Díaz. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oratorio_de_San_Felipe_Neri_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- Spain's parliament (the Cortes) met here from 1811, and here the 1812 Constitution, 'La Pepa', was proclaimed. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oratorio_de_San_Felipe_Neri_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- The 1755 Lisbon earthquake ruined its dome, which was rebuilt in 1764 with eight windows. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oratorio_de_San_Felipe_Neri_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- The plaques to the liberal 'doceañista' deputies went up mainly in 1912, for the Constitution's centenary. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oratorio_de_San_Felipe_Neri_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- Inside hangs a Murillo Immaculate Conception, considered one of his best. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oratorio_de_San_Felipe_Neri_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- *Note:* There may be plaques from other years, e.g. 2012, so check which years are visible. The interior needs a ticket.

**7. Plaza de Mina** · Plaza de Mina, 11004 Cádiz (house No. 3) · 36.5353031, -6.29690418

- The square was laid out in 1838 on the orchard and infirmary of the San Francisco convent. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Mina))
- It is named after General Espoz y Mina, a Peninsular War hero. The statue of him in the centre has since disappeared. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Mina))
- A plaque at No. 3 recalls that Manuel de Falla was born there on 23 Nov 1876. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Mina))
- The neoclassical building that houses part of the Museo de Cádiz opened as the Academy of Fine Arts on 10 Oct 1838. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Mina))
- In 1937 it was renamed Plaza del Generalísimo Franco, but everyone kept calling it Plaza de Mina. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Mina))
- *Note:* Shady gardens, a good rest stop. The Museo de Cádiz is on the square.

**8. Parque Genovés** · Main entrance on Plaza Rocío Jurado / Avenida Duque de Nájera, 11002 Cádiz · 36.53599797, -6.30401792

- Its first version, a modest late-18th-century walk, was nicknamed 'paseo del Perejil' (parsley walk). ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Genov%C3%A9s))
- It was enlarged from 1854, partly to give work to people in need during a cholera epidemic. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Genov%C3%A9s))
- Mayor Eduardo Genovés remodelled it in 1892 with fountains, a cascade lake, a concert hall and a café. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Genov%C3%A9s))
- A theatre with an iron frame by Gustave Eiffel once stood here. Around 1930 the frame was reused for the Mercado de la Merced, now the Flamenco Art School. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Genov%C3%A9s))
- The 'Niños bajo el paraguas' fountain came from Paris, and there is a Trafalgar monument with steel plates naming every ship in the battle. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Genov%C3%A9s))
- *Note:* The leg from Plaza de Mina is about 650 m along the Alameda Apodaca / Paseo Carlos III seafront. The park has opening hours. The cypresses are clipped into shapes, and children love La Gruta (a lake with a waterfall, ducks and geese).

</details>

### The Coplas Route · local

*From the land gate to the lighthouse: fishermen, carnival poets and the city's favourite beach.*

Puertas de Tierra → Castillo de San Sebastián causeway (lighthouse) · 7 stops · ≈ 3.8 km · 2.0 h · **needs a fix**

**Why:** It follows the working-class south and west edge of the old town (Santa María, Campo del Sur, La Viña), the cradle of carnival and of fishing life, and ends on the walk out to San Sebastián at sunset.

**Already offered:** Not verified: the web search budget ran out before checking for carnival or barrio walking tours in La Viña / Santa María.

**To fix:** 3 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Puertas de Tierra | Look up at the walls. What colour is the flag that flies over them? | Purple | sourced |
| 2 | Iglesia y convento de Santo Domingo | This church was built for Dominican friars travelling to and from America. Look at its bell tower: what is it mainly built of? | TBD on site | check |
| 3 | Teatro Romano de Cádiz (Teatro de Balbo) | People found this Roman theatre in 1980 by accident while searching for something else. What were they looking for? | A medieval castle | check |
| 4 | Barrio de La Viña | La Viña's name comes from what used to grow here before the houses. What? | Grapevines | check |
| 5 | La Caleta beach and the Paco Alba bust | On La Caleta there's a bust of a carnival legend, considered the inventor of the modern comparsa. Who is he? | Paco Alba | sourced |
| 6 | Castillo de Santa Catalina | Look at the castle's shape: the points sticking out towards the sea make it look like what? | A star | sourced |
| 7 | Castillo de San Sebastián causeway and lighthouse | The lighthouse inside San Sebastián castle is unusual for Spain. What is its structure made of? | Iron | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Puertas de Tierra** · Plaza de la Constitución, 11008 Cádiz (old-town side) · 36.52612984, -6.28873407

- The gate is what remains of the city's land wall. Its 18th-century form is by Torcuato Cayón, and the marble doorway is designed more like an altarpiece than a fortification. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_de_Tierra))
- A wall was first built here in the 16th century, and in 1574 it was extended with two bastions. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_de_Tierra))
- Its tower was No. 57 of Mathé's optical telegraph line from Madrid, working 1851-1857. On a clear day a message from Madrid arrived in about two hours. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_de_Tierra))
- In the 20th century the city considered demolishing it to let cars in. Instead two new arches were opened in the wall. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_de_Tierra))
- It divides 'Cádiz-Cádiz' (the old town) from 'Puerta Tierra' / 'Extramuros' (the new town), and a purple 'cantonal' flag flies over it. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_de_Tierra))
- *Note:* Check the flag is still flying (flags can be removed). Many buses stop here.

**2. Iglesia y convento de Santo Domingo** · Plaza de Santo Domingo, Barrio de Santa María, 11006 Cádiz · 36.528757, -6.290072

- It is Cádiz's church most linked to America: it was founded to house Dominicans travelling to and from the other side of the Atlantic. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convento_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_del_Rosario_y_Santo_Domingo_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- The convent was founded on 14 Jul 1635 at a spot called 'del boquete', where the Anglo-Dutch raiders of 1596 had thrown the image of the Virgin of the Rosary. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convento_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_del_Rosario_y_Santo_Domingo_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- The church was built from 21 Jun 1652 and finished by Luis de Lojo in 1666. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convento_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_del_Rosario_y_Santo_Domingo_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- Set on fire in 1931 and 1936, it was restored in 1945-1948 by Aurelio Gómez Millán. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convento_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_del_Rosario_y_Santo_Domingo_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- *Note:* No visible detail could be sourced. The question here is a placeholder and its options must be rewritten on site. Santa María is one of the oldest barrios (15th century) and traditionally a flamenco neighbourhood (flamenco link not sourced here). The Cigarreras brotherhood is based here.

**3. Teatro Romano de Cádiz (Teatro de Balbo)** · Calle Mesón 11-13, Barrio del Pópulo, 11005 Cádiz · 36.52842943, -6.29357312

- It was found in 1980 during excavations looking for the medieval Castillo de la Villa. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_romano_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- It was the second-largest theatre in Roman Hispania, after Córdoba's. The cavea (seating bowl) is 118 m across and held about 10,000 spectators. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_romano_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- It is considered the oldest Roman theatre found so far in the Iberian Peninsula. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_romano_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- It was abandoned in the 3rd century. The Moors built a citadel on its ruins, and Alfonso X enlarged it into the medieval castle. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_romano_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- *Note:* The answer can't be seen; the question only works if the interpretation centre's panels say so, or if it is rewritten for a visible panel. Whether entry is free is not confirmed, so check opening times. This stop touches the classic route's Pópulo area. The next leg follows the Campo del Sur sea wall (about 800 m, exposed).

**4. Barrio de La Viña** · Calle Virgen de la Palma, Barrio de La Viña, 11002 Cádiz · 36.52936, -6.30256

- The barrio's name comes from its past as a place where vines were grown. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Vi%C3%B1a_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- It was built up as the city's population boomed with the America trade. Too far from the port to interest the Indies merchants, it was settled by working people who lived off fishing at La Caleta beach. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Vi%C3%B1a_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- People from the barrio are called 'viñeros'. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Vi%C3%B1a_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- At carnival time, La Viña hosts the 'carrusel de coros', a parade of choirs singing from open-topped floats. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnaval_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- *Note:* The question can't be answered by looking. Replace it on site with a visible detail on Calle Virgen de la Palma, e.g. the Iglesia de la Palma façade or a carnival tile. The 1755 tsunami story about the Virgen de la Palma is popular but was not sourced, so it is left out. Lots of tapas bars.

**5. La Caleta beach and the Paco Alba bust** · Playa de La Caleta / Avenida Duque de Nájera, 11002 Cádiz · 36.53128, -6.30585

- A bust of carnival author Paco Alba stands at La Caleta. He is regarded as the inventor of the modern comparsa. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnaval_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- In 1975 Paco Alba's chirigota 'Los belloteros' won first prize, controversially in the comparsa category. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnaval_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- The Balneario de la Palma y del Real on the beach opened in 1926. Its two curved wings 'embrace' the sea, standing on thin pillars the high tide washes over. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balneario_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_la_Palma_y_del_Real))
- The balneario is now home to the Andalusian Underwater Archaeology Centre. Its only notable decoration is a tile panel of the Virgen de la Palma by Juan Ruiz de Luna. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balneario_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_la_Palma_y_del_Real))
- Carnival's choirs (coros) reached their first maturity with 'el Tío de la Tiza'. His 1905 tango 'Los duros antiguos' is carnival's unofficial anthem. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnaval_de_C%C3%A1diz))
- *Note:* The exact position of the bust is not sourced (only a Wikipedia photo caption places it at La Caleta), so find it on the walk. Crowded beach in summer. Sunset is the classic local moment here.

**6. Castillo de Santa Catalina** · Calle Antonio Burgos s/n (Paseo de La Caleta), 11002 Cádiz · 36.53285427, -6.30799044

- Philip II ordered it after the 1596 Anglo-Dutch raid, by Royal Decree of 25 Oct 1597. The engineer was Cristóbal de Rojas, who died before it was finished. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castillo_de_Santa_Catalina_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- Its plan is a pentagon in the Italian style, with star-like points jutting into the sea. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castillo_de_Santa_Catalina_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- From 1769 it was a military prison for notable inmates, including Mexican independence leader Mariano Abasolo, who died there in 1816, and some of those involved in the 23-F coup of 1981. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castillo_de_Santa_Catalina_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- The army stopped using it in 1991; it now hosts cultural events such as the Alcances film festival. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castillo_de_Santa_Catalina_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- *Note:* Usually free to enter, but check, since cultural-event days may differ. The star shape is easiest to see from the walls or the Caleta causeway.

**7. Castillo de San Sebastián causeway and lighthouse** · Paseo Fernando Quiñones (causeway from La Caleta), 11002 Cádiz · 36.52824536, -6.31597815

- By classical tradition, the islet held a temple of Moloch/Kronos. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castillo_de_San_Sebasti%C3%A1n_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- In 1457 crew from a Venetian ship, recovering from plague, built a hermitage on the islet. That gave the castle its name. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castillo_de_San_Sebasti%C3%A1n_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- The castle was begun in 1706. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castillo_de_San_Sebasti%C3%A1n_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- The lighthouse is an iron structure designed by Rafael de la Cerda in 1908, the only one of its kind in Spain and Spain's second electric lighthouse, 41 m above sea level. It was restored in 2017. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castillo_de_San_Sebasti%C3%A1n_(C%C3%A1diz)))
- *Note:* The causeway is open-air and free; the castle interior (a university marine lab) is not generally open. The causeway is very exposed in wind and at high seas. The last leg from Santa Catalina is about 900 m.

</details>


---

## Córdoba

**Visitors:** 833,659 visitors in 2022 (10th most popular Spanish city); the Mezquita-Catedral alone had a record 2.19 million visitors in 2025 (2022) ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba_%28Espa%C3%B1a%29)) *From Spanish Wikipedia; the city figure reflects hotel visitors (Observatorio Turístico data cited there). Not cross-checked.*

**Official walking routes:** [Turismo de Córdoba (city tourism body, domain cited on Wikipedia); route pages not checked](http://www.turismodecordoba.org)

**Notes:** Among the hottest cities in Spain: the airport record is 46.9 °C (14 July 2017 and 14 August 2021). Avoid midday walks from June to September. May is the peak: the Cruces de Mayo in early May, then the Festival de los Patios in the 2nd-3rd week (UNESCO intangible heritage since 2012), with big queues in Santa Marina and San Lorenzo, which affects the local route. Mostly flat; the local route has the Cuesta del Bailío steps. The Mezquita courtyard can close at service times. Source: https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba_%28Espa%C3%B1a%29. Research note: the web-search budget ran out, so official routes and competition are unverified.

### The Mezquita Route · classic

*Romans, a flowery lane, the orange-tree courtyard and a bridge from Game of Thrones.*

Roman Temple (Calle Claudio Marcelo) → Torre de la Calahorra · 8 stops · ≈ 2.6 km · 2.0 h · **needs a fix**

**Why:** Links Córdoba's Roman, Jewish, Islamic and Christian layers on one downhill line to the river, with no ticket needed.

**Already offered:** Not verified (the web-search budget ran out). This is the standard Judería/Mezquita circuit covered by most free tours; check turismodecordoba.org and the free-tour sites.

**To fix:** a 114 m leg (geofences may overlap); 1 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Roman Temple | The temple's columns and the pieces lying around it are carved from which stone? | Marble | sourced |
| 2 | Plaza del Potro: the colt fountain | What does the colt on top of the fountain hold with its front legs? | Córdoba's coat of arms (a shield) | sourced |
| 3 | Calleja de las Flores | What shape is the stone fountain in the little square at the end of the lane? | Octagonal | sourced |
| 4 | Statue of Maimonides, Judería | Is the bronze philosopher shown standing, sitting, on horseback or kneeling? | Sitting | likely |
| 5 | Patio de los Naranjos (Mezquita courtyard) | The courtyard's Fuente de Santa María has a pillar at each corner, each with a spout. How many spouts are there? | 4 | sourced |
| 6 | Triunfo de San Rafael and the Puerta del Puente | On the rocks of the monument, which bird holds a cartouche in its beak? | An eagle | sourced |
| 7 | Roman Bridge | Count the arches. How many does the bridge have today? | 16 | sourced |
| 8 | Torre de la Calahorra | A royal coat of arms from the 14th century is still visible on the outside. Which kingdoms does it belong to? | Castile and León | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Roman Temple** · Corner of Calle Claudio Marcelo and Calle Capitulares, Córdoba · 37.88476184, -4.7763862

- It was discovered in summer 1951, when works to extend the City Hall turned up Roman column shafts and capitals. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Templo_romano_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- Building began under Emperor Claudius (41-54) and finished about forty years later under Domitian (81-96). ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Templo_romano_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- It was a Corinthian temple 32 m long and 16 m wide, with six columns across the front and ten along each side, built almost entirely of marble. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Templo_romano_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- Several columns were put back up in the 1950s by Félix Hernández, using original pieces (anastylosis). The site opened to visitors on 19 June 2025. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Templo_romano_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- *Note:* Answer from the street railing. The site opened to the public in 2025; check whether entry is ticketed. Next to City Hall; buses on Calle Capitulares (check).

**2. Plaza del Potro: the colt fountain** · Plaza del Potro, Córdoba · 37.88115621, -4.77493608

- The Renaissance fountain was built in 1577 by the corregidor Garcí Suárez de Carvajal; the colt statue was added about a century later. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuente_del_Potro))
- In 1847 the fountain was moved here from the other side of the square, where a Triunfo de San Rafael now stands. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuente_del_Potro))
- The Posada del Potro inn at no. 10 is first recorded in 1381 and is mentioned in Cervantes' Don Quixote. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posada_del_Potro_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- Since 3 June 2013 the inn houses the Centro Flamenco Fosforito, named after the cantaor from Puente Genil. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posada_del_Potro_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- Anecdote: in 1964 Córdoba gave Jerez a replica of the colt when the two cities were twinned. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuente_del_Potro))
- *Note:* The Plaza de la Corredera (rectangular arcaded main square, 1683) is on the way from stop 1. It is not a stop because I found no good stable on-site detail for a question; mention it in the lesson. Source: https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_la_Corredera

**3. Calleja de las Flores** · Calleja de las Flores, off Calle Velázquez Bosco, Córdoba · 37.88038056, -4.77959167

- Anecdote: the flowerpots started in September 1939, when Felisa, Rafaela Tena's mother, moved in at no. 2 with hundreds of pots and, short of space, put the rest in the street. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calleja_de_las_Flores_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- Its present look dates from a mid-1950s project by architect Víctor Escribano Ucelay: little arches, river-pebble paving and a caliphal capital at the corner. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calleja_de_las_Flores_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- The octagonal fountain was made in 1960 by neighbour Rafael Bernier, who built in archaeological pieces such as a Roman column. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calleja_de_las_Flores_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- From the little square you get the classic framed view of the Mezquita's bell tower. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calleja_de_las_Flores_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- *Note:* It is a narrow dead-end lane and gets jammed with groups at midday, so keep the GPS radius tight because the lane is short. The pots are best in spring.

**4. Statue of Maimonides, Judería** · Plaza de Tiberíades, Judería, Córdoba · 37.879399, -4.783218

- The statue in the Plaza de Tiberíades is of Maimonides, the Jewish philosopher born in Córdoba. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juder%C3%ADa_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- When the Almohads arrived in 1148 they banned Jewish life in the region, and Maimonides had to go into exile with his family. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juder%C3%ADa_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- The nearby synagogue reopened in 1985 for the 850th anniversary of Maimonides' birth. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinagoga_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- The synagogue closed for restoration on 19 December 2017 and reopened on 30 October 2018. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinagoga_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- *Note:* No source coordinates found. My answer that he is seated is from memory, so it is 'likely'; verify on the walk. The Synagogue is a few metres away on Calle Judíos (access and fees not checked).

**5. Patio de los Naranjos (Mezquita courtyard)** · Patio de los Naranjos, Mezquita-Catedral, Calle Cardenal Herrero, Córdoba (via the Puerta del Perdón) · 37.87886543, -4.77934163

- The courtyard began as the ablutions courtyard of Abd al-Rahman I's mosque; it is about 130 m long and 50 m wide, with six doors. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezquita-catedral_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- Orange trees are recorded here from 1512. Today there are also ten palms, seven cypresses, two olives and a chinaberry tree. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezquita-catedral_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- Anecdote: the foundations of Hisham I's first minaret were found in this courtyard and are marked in the paving. See if you can find them. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezquita-catedral_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- The Baroque fountain dates from around 1741; one spout is nicknamed 'caño del olivo' after the near-thousand-year-old olive tree beside it, since replanted. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezquita-catedral_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- In 2025 the Mezquita-Catedral had a record 2.19 million visitors. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezquita-catedral_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- *Note:* Coordinates are the Wikipedia point for the whole Mezquita; the courtyard is its northern part, so the real walk to stop 6 is longer than the 114 m straight line between the two points (out of the courtyard and round the west or east side, roughly 250 m; check). The courtyard is normally free to enter, but the prayer hall is ticketed and the courtyard can close at times (check opening hours). Very crowded.

**6. Triunfo de San Rafael and the Puerta del Puente** · Plaza del Triunfo, beside the Puerta del Puente, Córdoba · 37.87785584, -4.77958043

- The monument to the archangel Raphael, the city's guardian, was begun in 1765 and finished in 1781 under the French sculptor Miguel Verdiguier; it is over 27 m tall. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triunfo_de_San_Rafael_%28Puerta_del_Puente%29))
- Look for the white marble statues of Saints Acisclo, Victoria and Barbara, a white stone lion, a life-size horse and a piece of artillery. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triunfo_de_San_Rafael_%28Puerta_del_Puente%29))
- Anecdote: the eagle's cartouche reads 'I swear to you by Christ Crucified that I am Raphael, the angel whom God has set as guardian of this city'. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triunfo_de_San_Rafael_%28Puerta_del_Puente%29))
- The Renaissance Puerta del Puente was begun in 1572 to mark Philip II's Cortes of 1570. Many locals wrongly call it the 'arco del triunfo'. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_del_Puente_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- *Note:* Open square next to the bridge and the visitor centre. Railings around the monument carry ten Latin inscriptions.

**7. Roman Bridge** · Puente Romano, Córdoba (across the Guadalquivir from the Puerta del Puente) · 37.87662548, -4.7778841

- The bridge is about 331 m long and has 16 arches, though it originally had 17. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_romano_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- For twenty centuries it was the city's only bridge, until the San Rafael bridge was built in the mid-20th century. It has been pedestrian-only since 1 May 2004. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_romano_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- Despite its name, only arches 14 and 15 (counting from the Puerta del Puente) are original; some arches are pointed, a medieval form. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_romano_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- The statue of Saint Raphael in the middle (1651, by Bernabé Gómez del Río) is the oldest of the city's Triunfos. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_romano_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- Anecdote: in 2014 it was used to film the Long Bridge of Volantis for season 5 of Game of Thrones. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_romano_de_C%C3%B3rdoba))
- *Note:* Arches are easiest to count from the riverbank (either end) rather than on the deck. Note this in the riddle. Busy at sunset.

**8. Torre de la Calahorra** · Torre de la Calahorra, south end of the Puente Romano, Córdoba · 37.87564901, -4.77658854

- The fortress guarded the south end of the bridge; it is first mentioned during Fernando III's conquest of Córdoba in 1236. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_de_la_Calahorra_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- King Enrique II added a moat and a second tower linked by a drawbridge; the arms of Castile and León date from then. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_de_la_Calahorra_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- In 1514 Queen Juana I ordered a barbican around it, giving it the shape you see today. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_de_la_Calahorra_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- It has been a prison for nobles, a girls' school (1863) and a Guardia Civil barracks; since 1987 it houses the Museo Vivo de al-Ándalus. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_de_la_Calahorra_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- *Note:* The museum is ticketed, so answer from outside. Exactly which wall carries the shield is not confirmed; check on the walk. End point: good view back over the bridge to the Mezquita.

</details>

### The Bullfighters' Quarter Route · local

*Legends, lanterns and the 'Fernandine' churches of the old Axerquía.*

Torre de la Malmuerta → Basílica de San Pedro (near Plaza de la Corredera) · 7 stops · ≈ 3.0 km · 2.0 h · **needs a fix**

**Why:** Santa Marina and San Lorenzo are lived-in barrios of patios, churches and legends that most day-trippers never reach.

**Already offered:** Not verified (search budget ran out). Fernandine-church routes and patio tours exist; check before launch.

**To fix:** 2 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Torre de la Malmuerta | How many sides does the tower have? | 8 | sourced |
| 2 | Santa Marina church and the Manolete monument | How many big buttresses divide the main façade of Santa Marina church? | 4 | sourced |
| 3 | Cristo de los Faroles, Plaza de Capuchinos | How many lanterns surround the Christ? | 8 | sourced |
| 4 | Iglesia de San Agustín | Look at the church tower. How many tiers of bell openings does it have? | 2 | likely |
| 5 | Iglesia de San Lorenzo | How many pointed arches open in the porch in front of the main door? | 3 | sourced |
| 6 | Iglesia de San Andrés | In the niche above the Baroque main door, Saint Andrew carries his cross. What shape is it? | X-shaped | sourced |
| 7 | Basílica de San Pedro | Above the main façade, which feature of the original medieval church was kept? | A rose window | likely |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Torre de la Malmuerta** · Torre de la Malmuerta, barrio de Santa Marina, Córdoba (by the Plaza de Colón; exact spot to verify) · 37.89173113, -4.77765773

- The tower was built between 1404 and 1408, ordered by King Enrique III, on the remains of a Muslim tower, to defend the Rincón and Colodro gates. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_de_la_Malmuerta_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
- It stands on an arch over the old wall; under the arch are the royal arms and an almost erased inscription. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_de_la_Malmuerta_%28C%C3%B3rdoba%29))
