## Segovia

**Visitors:** No verified city-wide tourist total found. Best proxies: Segovia Cathedral had 562,848 visitors in 2025, its highest on record (36,919 more than 2024). The city tourism office publishes annual reports (Memoria 2024, Observatorio Turístico) with traveller and overnight data, but their totals could not be read from search summaries.

**Official walking routes:** [Del Acueducto al Alcázar (tourism office route leaflet)](https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2022-10/DEL%20ACUEDUCTO%20AL%20ALCAZAR.pdf); [El Valle del Eresma (tourism office route leaflet)](https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2022-10/EL%20VALLE%20DEL%20ERESMA.pdf); [Paseos verdes por la ciudad de Segovia (2026)](https://www.turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2026-09/2026%20Paseos%20verdes%20por%20la%20ciudad%20de%20Segovia.pdf); [Miradores en Segovia (inside / outside viewpoints routes)](https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2022-10/Miradores%20en%20Segovia.pdf); [Ruta del Románico](https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2020-01/Ruta-romanico.pdf); [La Judería de Segovia](https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2025-01/La%20Juderia%20de%20Segovia_0.pdf); [Ruta San Juan de la Cruz en Segovia](https://www.turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2026-06/San%20Juan%20de%20la%20Cruz%20en%20Segovia.pdf); [Geological walk 'Date una vuelta de 600 millones de años'](https://www.turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2020-02/1.%20Date%20una%20vuelta%20de%20600%20M.a.pdf); [Walks around Segovia (English page; bespoke tour help)](https://www.turismodesegovia.com/en/walks-around-segovia)

**Notes:** City sits at roughly 1,000 m altitude: cold, sometimes icy winters and strong sun in summer; carry water. Very heavy day-tripper crowds from Madrid at the Azoguejo, Calle Real (Cervantes/Juan Bravo) and Plaza Mayor around midday and at weekends; start early. The old town is on a rock spur: expect climbs from the Azoguejo up Calle Real and steep descents/climbs to the Eresma and Clamores valleys (the local route goes down to the river and back up). Cobbles throughout. Local fiestas of San Juan y San Pedro in late June fill the centre (confirm dates yearly). turismodesegovia.com could not be fetched from this environment (DNS blocked), so official pages were read only via search summaries. Some fact-to-URL attributions come from search summaries rather than opened pages; stop notes flag the doubtful ones.

### The Aqueduct-to-Alcázar Route · classic

*Follow the Roman water line up through the walls to the castle at the prow of the city.*

Iglesia de San Millán, Avenida del Acueducto (about 5 min walk from the bus station) → Alcázar de Segovia, Plaza de la Reina Victoria Eugenia · 8 stops · ≈ 2.5 km · 2.5 h · **needs a fix**

**Why:** It strings Segovia's three UNESCO icons (aqueduct, cathedral, Alcázar) along the old main street in one uphill line, with a quiet Romanesque church as a warm-up.

**Already offered:** Yes. The tourism office publishes an 'Del Acueducto al Alcázar' leaflet covering essentially this axis, and most free/paid walking tours follow it. https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2022-10/DEL%20ACUEDUCTO%20AL%20ALCAZAR.pdf

**To fix:** a 113 m leg (geofences may overlap)

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Iglesia de San Millán | This church is wrapped in a covered porch of stone arches. On how many sides of the church does the arcaded porch run? | 2 | sourced |
| 2 | Aqueduct at Plaza del Azoguejo | At the foot of the aqueduct stands a bronze statue given by the city of Rome. Which animal is it? | A she-wolf | sourced |
| 3 | Casa de los Picos | What covers the whole front of this house? | Pointed granite 'diamond' studs | sourced |
| 4 | Plaza de Medina del Campo (Juan Bravo monument) | Two stone creatures, which locals call 'sirenas', guard this square near the statue of Juan Bravo. How many are there? | 2 | sourced |
| 5 | Plaza Mayor | Which part of the cathedral rises over the Plaza Mayor? | Its rounded east end (the apse) | sourced |
| 6 | Cathedral west front (Puerta del Perdón) | Which figure presides over the cathedral's main west door, the Puerta del Perdón? | The Virgin Mary | sourced |
| 7 | Iglesia de San Esteban (tower) | Look at the very top of the tall Romanesque tower. What metal figure sits on the weather vane? | A rooster | sourced |
| 8 | Alcázar of Segovia (Plaza de la Reina Victoria Eugenia) | In the square in front of the Alcázar stands a monument to two artillery officers. Who are they? | Daoíz and Velarde | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Iglesia de San Millán** · Avenida del Acueducto / Calle Fernández Ladreda, barrio de San Millán, Segovia · 40.945632, -4.120724

- It was built in 1111-1123 by King Alfonso I 'the Battler' of Aragon on the site of an earlier pre-Romanesque church, with influences from Jaca Cathedral. ([source](https://www.terranostrum.es/turismo/iglesia-de-san-millan-segovia))
- Arcaded porches (atrios) are a trademark of Segovian Romanesque; at San Millán the porch wraps around two sides of the church. ([source](https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2020-01/Ruta-romanico.pdf))
- The bell tower survives from the earlier Mozarabic-style building: look for its horseshoe-arched windows, topped later by a Baroque spire. ([source](https://www.terranostrum.es/turismo/iglesia-de-san-millan-segovia))
- It was declared a protected monument (today Bien de Interés Cultural) on 3 June 1931. ([source](https://www.terranostrum.es/turismo/iglesia-de-san-millan-segovia))
- *Note:* Sources disagree on the tower's date (10th century Mozarabic vs 11th century pre-Romanesque), so no date is given in facts. Dates for the church vary slightly (1111-1123 vs 1111-1124) and some sources call the royal patronage only 'probable'. Porch is visible from the street; interior opening is irregular. Quiet start away from crowds.

**2. Aqueduct at Plaza del Azoguejo** · Plaza del Azoguejo, Segovia · 40.94788889, -4.11780556

- The arcade has 167 arches and carried water from mountain springs about 17 km away; it was in use until 1973. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqueduct_of_Segovia))
- The aqueduct is on Segovia's coat of arms, and the old town and aqueduct became a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1985. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqueduct_of_Segovia))
- The she-wolf suckling Romulus and Remus is a bronze copy of Rome's Capitoline Wolf, donated by Rome in 1974 for the aqueduct's bimillennium. ([source](https://soulbank.com/?p=4672))
- A Virgin statue stood in one of the niches high on the arches from 1520 until 2015 (a replica is there now); the original, about 800 kg and 1.86 m tall, is shown in the Aqueduct Interpretation Centre at the Real Casa de Moneda. The other niche held Saint Sebastian. ([source](https://www.turismodesegovia.com/es/acueducto/visitas-guiadas-y-actividades-para-grupos/centro-de-interpretacion-del-acueducto-visita))
- *Note:* Busiest spot in the city; coach groups all day. Sources disagree on the construction date (Spanish Wikipedia: early 2nd century AD under Trajan/Hadrian; English Wikipedia: around the 1st century AD), on the maximum height (28 m, 28.1 m, 28.5 m, 30 m) and on which advocation the niche Virgin is (Fuencisla vs Carmen vs none) and whether the second niche is San Sebastián (tourism office) or San Esteban (a hotel guide), so these are left out of facts. Leg from San Millán runs right beside the arches.

**3. Casa de los Picos** · Calle Juan Bravo 33, Segovia · 40.947996, -4.12058

- The house takes its name from the granite points (picos) that cover its façade. ([source](https://www.idealista.com/news/node/839747))
- It houses Segovia's School of Art and Higher School of Design, which has occupied it since 1977. ([source](https://lamaletainquieta.com/casa-de-los-picos-segovia/))
- You can admire the façade from the street any time; the inside opens only during exhibitions. ([source](https://lamaletainquieta.com/casa-de-los-picos-segovia/))
- It stands on Calle Juan Bravo, in the heart of the historic centre. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_de_los_Picos))
- *Note:* Guides repeat a count of 617 picos, but one source points out that no official body publishes it, so the number is not used as an answer. One listing gives the address as Juan Bravo 5; Juan Bravo 33 is used by spain.info and the Castilla y León tourism listing. Calle Real is uphill and crowded.

**4. Plaza de Medina del Campo (Juan Bravo monument)** · Plaza de Medina del Campo, off Calle Juan Bravo, next to Iglesia de San Martín, Segovia · 40.9487, -4.121546

- The statue of the comunero leader Juan Bravo is by the Segovian sculptor Aniceto Marinas (1921). ([source](https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2025-03/plano-segovia.pdf))
- The square's name honours Medina del Campo for supporting Segovia during the Comuneros revolt. ([source](https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2025-03/plano-segovia.pdf))
- Juan Bravo was one of the leaders of the Revolt of the Comuneros against Charles V and was executed after the defeat at Villalar in 1521. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Bravo))
- The two sphinx-like 'sirenas' are by the sculptor Francisco Bellver. ([source](https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2025-03/plano-segovia.pdf))
- *Note:* Sources disagree on the sirens' date (1851 vs 1852), so it is left out. Alternative question (lower confidence): Juan Bravo holds a flag in his right hand (visitor guide only). The square is also called Plaza de San Martín or 'de las sirenas'. Steps between the upper and lower parts of the square.

**5. Plaza Mayor** · Plaza Mayor, Segovia · 40.95029722, -4.12397778

- The square holds the apse of the cathedral, the Teatro Juan Bravo and the town hall. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Mayor_de_Segovia))
- Segovia Cathedral is nicknamed 'the Lady of Cathedrals' for its size and elegance. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_Segovia))
- It was built in 1525-1577, making it one of the last Gothic cathedrals in Spain and Europe, at a time when most of Europe was building in Renaissance style. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_Segovia))
- *Note:* Café terraces and markets can block sightlines. The apse is the obvious view from the square, so the question is easy but kid-friendly.

**6. Cathedral west front (Puerta del Perdón)** · Calle Marqués del Arco / Plaza de la Catedral, Segovia · 40.950181, -4.125623

- The main façade faces west and is called the Puerta del Perdón; a statue of the Virgin stands on it. ([source](https://turismodesegovia.com/en/monuments/17-cathedral))
- It was built in three stages; the first (1525-1557) was led by the architect Juan Gil de Hontañón, his son Rodrigo and García de Cubillas. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_Segovia))
- Its full name is the Cathedral of Our Lady of the Assumption and Saint Frutos, Segovia's patron. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_Segovia))
- *Note:* Coordinates are Wikipedia's for the whole cathedral, not the door. Entry to the interior is ticketed; the question uses only the outside. The tourism office credits the Virgin to Juan Guas, but Guas worked on the old cathedral (1473-1491), so the attribution is doubtful and left out of facts. Walk around the cathedral from the Plaza Mayor (about 250-300 m on foot).

**7. Iglesia de San Esteban (tower)** · Plaza de San Esteban, Segovia · 40.95198056, -4.12488333

- The church was built in the 12th century and is known for its slender tower. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_San_Esteban_(Segovia)))
- With its old Baroque spire the tower reached 56 m and was the tallest Romanesque bell tower in the Iberian Peninsula; today's roof, a few decades old, brings it to about 50 m. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_San_Esteban_(Segovia)))
- It and the tower of Santa María la Antigua in Valladolid are called 'queens of the Romanesque towers of Castile'. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_San_Esteban_(Segovia)))
- The tower was added in the early 13th century to the 12th-century church. ([source](https://www.urbipedia.org/hoja/Iglesia_de_San_Esteban_(Segovia)))
- *Note:* Sources disagree on the height today (about 50 m vs 53 m), the number of storeys (five vs six) and the date of the lightning fire that destroyed the spire (1894 vs 1896), so these are left out. The rooster may be hard to see in strong backlight; consider binoculars hint.

**8. Alcázar of Segovia (Plaza de la Reina Victoria Eugenia)** · Plaza de la Reina Victoria Eugenia, Segovia · 40.9525, -4.1325

- The Alcázar dates from the early 12th century and has been a Roman fort site, medieval fortress, royal palace, treasury, state prison, artillery college and military archive. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alc%C3%A1zar_de_Segovia))
- The monument to Daoíz and Velarde, by sculptor Aniceto Marinas, was unveiled on 15 July 1910 by King Alfonso XIII. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_to_Daoiz_and_Velarde_(Segovia)))
- Both officers trained as cadets at the Royal Artillery College in the Alcázar and died fighting French troops on 2 May 1808. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_to_Daoiz_and_Velarde_(Segovia)))
- The big crenellated keep facing the square is the Tower of Juan II, named after the king who started it. ([source](https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2025-03/plano-segovia.pdf))
- *Note:* Interior and tower are ticketed; the square and moat-side views are free. The monument was due for restoration/cleaning by the IPCE (announced in the 2024 Día del Alcázar report), so check for scaffolding. Long leg from San Esteban along Calle Daoíz/Velarde (about 650 m straight line).

</details>

### The Eresma Valley Route · local

*Drop below the walls to monks, mills, a royal mint, a 12-sided church and a legend of a woman who fell from the cliffs.*

Plaza de San Lorenzo (barrio de San Lorenzo) → Santuario de la Fuencisla (Alameda de la Fuencisla) · 6 stops · ≈ 2.6 km · 2.0 h · **ready to write**

**Why:** Most visitors only see Segovia from on top of its rock; this walk circles the foot of it through the riverside suburbs where Segovians walk, with monasteries, industry and Jewish and Christian legends.

**Already offered:** Partly. The tourism office publishes 'El Valle del Eresma' and 'Paseos verdes' leaflets and the 'Senda de los Molinos'; a 10 km circuit through San Lorenzo, Parral, Casa de Moneda, Vera Cruz and Fuencisla also exists. No treasure-hunt format found. https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2022-10/EL%20VALLE%20DEL%20ERESMA.pdf

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Plaza de San Lorenzo | The church tower gets narrower as it rises but gains windows. How many windows are there on each side of the top level? | 4 | sourced |
| 2 | Monasterio de Santa Cruz la Real | The façade still carries the famous motto of the Catholic Monarchs, Isabella and Ferdinand. What does it say? | Tanto monta | sourced |
| 3 | Monasterio de Santa María del Parral | High up on the unfinished west front are big coats of arms of the Pacheco family. How many are there? | 2 | sourced |
| 4 | Real Casa de Moneda (Royal Mint) | The machines of this royal mint struck coins without horses or steam. What powered them? | River water turning wheels | sourced |
| 5 | Iglesia de la Vera Cruz | Walk around this unusual church. How many sides does its main body have? | 12 | sourced |
| 6 | Santuario de Nuestra Señora de la Fuencisla | Next to the sanctuary is the Arco de la Fuencisla. Its inner side shows the miracle of a young Jewish woman. What is she called? | María del Salto | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Plaza de San Lorenzo** · Plaza de San Lorenzo, Segovia · 40.95279167, -4.11405278

- San Lorenzo is a Romanesque-Mudéjar church of the 13th century, built over an earlier Mozarabic one, and stands completely free in its square. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_San_Lorenzo_(Segovia)))
- Its tower narrows as it gains height while its windows multiply, from one to four on each side. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_San_Lorenzo_(Segovia)))
- The carved capitals of its three apses show Bible scenes, including a very expressive martyrdom of Saint Lawrence (who was roasted on a grill). ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_San_Lorenzo_(Segovia)))
- It was declared a national historic-artistic monument on 3 June 1931, during the Second Republic. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_San_Lorenzo_(Segovia)))
- *Note:* Tourism sources call the tower brick and give a different number of levels (five), and date the nave to the 12th century; Wikipedia says 13th century. The square is surrounded by old half-timbered houses (tourism office). Served by urban buses. Quiet residential square.

**2. Monasterio de Santa Cruz la Real** · Calle Cardenal Zúñiga, Segovia (IE University campus) · 40.952899, -4.118797

- It was the first Dominican monastery in Spain, founded by Saint Dominic (Santo Domingo de Guzmán) himself in 1218 on houses given by the city. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convento_de_Santa_Cruz_la_Real))
- The Catholic Monarchs rebuilt it and gave it a relic of the True Cross that Boabdil, the last king of Granada, had given them; that is when it earned the title 'Real'. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convento_de_Santa_Cruz_la_Real))
- Its church is the work of Juan Guas, and the façade still keeps the monarchs' motto 'Tanto monta'. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convento_de_Santa_Cruz_la_Real))
- Today its buildings are the Segovia campus of IE University. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convento_de_Santa_Cruz_la_Real))
- *Note:* Backup question: a tourism leaflet says the doorway of the chapel at the Cave of Santo Domingo shows the founder hugging a cross with two foxes at his feet (https://turismodesegovia.com/sites/default/files/Media/Files/2020-01/Traslahuelladelospredicadores.pdf); check it is visible from outside. Exact street number not confirmed. Check where 'Tanto monta' sits on the façade so the GPS point is right.

**3. Monasterio de Santa María del Parral** · Calle del Marqués de Villena, Alameda del Parral, Segovia · 40.95602798, -4.1265831

- Founded by King Enrique IV in 1447 and protected by his favourite Juan Pacheco, Marquis of Villena, who placed his arms on the unfinished portal. ([source](https://www.spain.info/es/lugares-interes/monasterio-santa-maria-parral/))
- It is an enclosed monastery of the Order of Saint Jerome (Hieronymites). ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasterio_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_del_Parral))
- The west façade was begun in 1529 by Juan Campero and its portal sculpture by Sebastián de Almonacid was never completed. ([source](https://www.terranostrum.es/turismo/monasterio-santa-maria-del-parral-segovia))
- *Note:* Leg from San Lorenzo is about 1.1 km straight line along the Eresma; with the Santa Cruz stop in between it splits into two legs of roughly 0.6-0.7 km each, but measure on the ground. Sources disagree on the founding year and who started it (1445 vs 1447; legend credits Pacheco), so only the spain.info version is used. Interior visits are limited (monks).

**4. Real Casa de Moneda (Royal Mint)** · Calle de la Moneda, Segovia (across the Eresma from El Parral) · 40.954318, -4.128518

- It was built between 1583 and 1586 by Philip II's royal architect Juan de Herrera, founded and sponsored by the king himself. ([source](https://www.turismocastillayleon.com/es/arte-cultura-patrimonio/monumentos/edificios/real-casa-moneda))
- Herrera's plans turned an old paper mill on the Eresma into the new coin factory. ([source](https://www.turismocastillayleon.com/en/heritage-culture/buildings/real-casa-moneda))
- It closed for good in 1868, together with the Seville mint, after Madrid opened a large steam-powered mint in 1861. ([source](https://www.ecured.cu/Real_Ingenio_de_Segovia))
- Today it houses a museum and the Aqueduct Interpretation Centre, which displays the original 1520 Virgin from the aqueduct niche. ([source](https://www.turismodesegovia.com/es/acueducto/visitas-guiadas-y-actividades-para-grupos/centro-de-interpretacion-del-acueducto-visita))
- *Note:* Sources disagree on the number of water wheels (14, 9, or 8 in the first building) and on how Philip II got the site (bought a paper mill vs gift), so these are left out of facts. Some sources say the first machines came from the Hall mint near Innsbruck as a gift from Archduke Ferdinand of Tyrol; only a wiki (EcuRed) supports this, so it is left out. The question is about the visible weir and wheel channels; the museum itself is ticketed. Confirm exact street address on the walk.

**5. Iglesia de la Vera Cruz** · Carretera de Zamarramala, barrio de San Marcos, Segovia · 40.955712, -4.13227

- Its exterior is polygonal with twelve sides, a projecting square tower and simple doorways. ([source](https://www.turismocastillayleon.com/es/patrimonio-cultura/iglesias-ermitas/iglesia-vera-cruz-6339))
- It was consecrated on 13 April 1208, as recorded by an inscription on a white stone over the south doorway. ([source](https://www.ucm.es/archivoepigraficohispania/file/bae2aehtam3038inscripcionfundacionaldel))
- It was first called the church of the Holy Sepulchre ('Beati Sepulcri' in the inscription) and stands on the slope up to Zamarramala, whose parish church it was for centuries. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_la_Vera_Cruz_(Segovia)))
- Popular tradition links it to the Knights Templar, but a 1919 study argued it was never Templar and was founded for the Order of the Holy Sepulchre. ([source](https://coam.org/media/Default%20Files/fundacion/biblioteca/revista-arquitectura-100/1918-1931/docs/revista-articulos/revista-arquitectura-1919-n14-pag165-169.pdf))
- *Note:* Interior ticketed and closed some days; the question uses only the outside. Uphill leg from the Casa de Moneda (about 600-700 m); Great view of the Alcázar from here. The Convento de San Juan de la Cruz (tomb of the poet-saint; Carmelites bought the building in 1586 and he founded the convent in 1588, per the tourism office; outdoor statue of the saint by José María García Moro) is only about 90 m away in a straight line, too close for its own stop, so mention it in the lesson or use it as a swap stop.

**6. Santuario de Nuestra Señora de la Fuencisla** · Alameda de la Fuencisla, at the foot of the Peñas Grajeras, Segovia · 40.9565888, -4.1352613

- The sanctuary houses Segovia's patron, the Virgin of Fuencisla. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santuario_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_la_Fuencisla))
- It is a 17th-century Herrerian church to a 1598 design by Francisco de Mora, with 18th-century ironwork donated by the wool carders' guild. ([source](https://www.turismocastillayleon.com/es/patrimonio-cultura/iglesias-ermitas/santuario-senora-fuencisla))
- Legend says a Jewish woman wrongly accused of adultery was thrown from the Peñas Grajeras cliffs above, survived, was baptised and became known as María del Salto ('Mary of the Leap'). ([source](https://www.cervantesvirtual.com/obra-visor/espana-sus-monumentos-y-artes-su-naturaleza-e-historia-tomo-3--0/html/fefc02c4-82b1-11df-acc7-002185ce6064_112.html))
- The monumental Arco de la Fuencisla was built around 1700: Saint Ferdinand (King Fernando III) is on its outer face and a relief of the María del Salto miracle on the inside. ([source](https://turismodesegovia.com/en/monuments/66-fuencisla-arch-arco-de-la-fuencisla))
- *Note:* Sources disagree about the first shrine (a 1237 date appears once, unverified) and about where María del Salto is buried (old cathedral vs cloister inscription). Leg from the Vera Cruz is about 270 m straight line, downhill. End of route: the way back to the centre is a climb (about 15 min to the Alcázar via the Cuesta de los Hoyos or Puerta de Santiago). Optional extension along the Clamores valley to the Peñas Grajeras and the free Old Jewish Cemetery on El Pinarillo hill (body-shaped rock-cut graves; about 1.1 km straight line, too long for one leg, so add a midpoint if used).

</details>


---

## Sevilla

**Visitors:** 7.4 million overnight stays (pernoctaciones) in 2025; described as the 4th most-visited city in Spain after Barcelona, Madrid and Benidorm (2025) ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sevilla)) *From Spanish Wikipedia; not cross-checked with INE or the city tourism observatory (the search budget ran out).*

**Official walking routes:** ['In the quarter of Alfalfa' (Visita Sevilla, legacy site; French-language page)](https://legacy.visitasevilla.es/fr/node/450); [IAPH route brochure: 'Recorriendo calles de Sevilla: artistas del Siglo de Oro' (street furniture and monuments)](https://repositorio.iaph.es/bitstream/11532/326751/4/Ruta_folleto_recorriendo_calles_sevilla_artistas_siglo_oro_mobiliario_urbano.pdf)

**Notes:** Flat city, easy walking. Summers are extremely hot: the AEMET record at the airport is 47.4 °C (6 Aug 1946), so schedule routes in early morning or evening from June to September. Holy Week (Semana Santa) and the Feria de Abril bring huge crowds, and processions block streets in the centre and in Triana. Triana's Velá de Santiago y Santa Ana fills Calle Betis in late July. Source for climate and festivals: https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sevilla ; https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_Betis. Research note: the web-search budget ran out partway through, so official routes and competition are not verified, and most facts come from Spanish Wikipedia.

### The Giralda Route · classic

*From giant wooden mushrooms to the grandest square in Spain.*

Setas de Sevilla, Plaza de la Encarnación → Plaza de España · 8 stops · ≈ 3.1 km · 2.5 h · **needs a fix**

**Why:** One line through Seville's must-sees: Setas, City Hall, Cathedral and Giralda, Santa Cruz and Plaza de España, all answerable from the street.

**Already offered:** Not verified: the web-search budget ran out before I could check. This is the standard first-day line, so expect free walking tours and audio guides covering it. Check visitasevilla.es and the big free-tour sites.

**To fix:** a 123 m leg (geofences may overlap)

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Setas de Sevilla (Metropol Parasol) | How many giant 'mushroom' parasols make up the wooden canopy? | 6 | sourced |
| 2 | Plaza del Salvador: statue of Martínez Montañés | The seated bronze statue in the square honours a man of which profession? | Sculptor | sourced |
| 3 | Plaza de San Francisco and City Hall | Which Roman god stands on top of the fountain in the square? | Mercury | sourced |
| 4 | Plaza del Triunfo | The Immaculate Conception monument has statues of four famous Sevillians. Which painter is one of them? | Murillo | sourced |
| 5 | The Giralda, from Plaza Virgen de los Reyes | Look at the bronze figure turning on top of the Giralda. What does she hold? | A shield and a palm branch | sourced |
| 6 | Plaza de Santa Cruz | Look at the base of the wrought-iron cross in the middle of the garden. How many lanterns sit on it? | 4 | sourced |
| 7 | Columbus Monument, Jardines de Murillo | What animal stands on top of the monument? | A lion | sourced |
| 8 | Plaza de España | How many bridges cross the canal (ría) in front of the building? | 4 | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Setas de Sevilla (Metropol Parasol)** · Plaza de la Encarnación, Sevilla · 37.39325278, -5.99188056

- Designed by German architect Jürgen Mayer, chosen from 65 entries in a city competition, and completed in April 2011. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropol_Parasol))
- It measures roughly 150 x 70 m and about 26 m high, and is built from 3,500 cubic metres of micro-laminated Finnish pine; it is marketed as the world's largest wooden structure. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropol_Parasol))
- When the city first tried to build a market and car park here in the 1990s, Roman and al-Andalus ruins turned up and work was frozen after 14 million euros had been spent. The ruins are now shown in the underground Antiquarium. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropol_Parasol))
- Anecdote: locals nicknamed it 'las setas' (the mushrooms). The city made 'Setas de Sevilla' the official name after finding the architect had trademarked 'Metropol Parasol' and would charge for its use. ([source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropol_Parasol))
- *Note:* Count the parasols from street level in the plaza (no ticket). The rooftop walkway and the Antiquarium are ticketed; the question does not need them. Market at street level. Check the nearest bus stops for the route start.

**2. Plaza del Salvador: statue of Martínez Montañés** · Plaza del Salvador, Sevilla (in front of the Iglesia del Salvador) · 37.38995833, -5.99326389

- The monument depicts Juan Martínez Montañés (1568-1649), born in Alcalá la Real, one of the greatest masters of the Seville school of sculpture. ([source](https://andalucia.org/en/sevilla-cultural-tourism-monumento-a-juan-martinez-montanes))
- The statue was moved in 1967 to a square beside the cathedral and returned to the Plaza del Salvador in 1985, its definitive home. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Salvador_%28Sevilla%29))
- The Iglesia del Salvador is a Baroque church and the second-largest church in the city after the cathedral. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Salvador_%28Sevilla%29))
- The square was once called Plaza del Cementerio because it held part of the parish cemetery; a wooden cross allowed in 1608 still stands at the corner with Calle Villegas. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Salvador_%28Sevilla%29))
- Anecdote: in 2013 the square appeared in episode 8 of the US series Agents of S.H.I.E.L.D. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Salvador_%28Sevilla%29))
- *Note:* Busy bar terraces at midday and evening. Sources disagree on the year the statue was first installed (1923 vs 1924), so it is left out of the facts. The IAPH route brochure describes the figure as sitting in a bronze chair.

**3. Plaza de San Francisco and City Hall** · Plaza de San Francisco, Sevilla (east façade of the Ayuntamiento) · 37.38853333, -5.99399167

- The City Hall is the first Plateresque-style building made in Spain; work began in late 1526 under master builder Diego de Riaño. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_consistorial_de_Sevilla))
- The city's motto NO8DO is read 'NO - madeja (skein of wool) - DO', i.e. 'no me ha dejado' ('it has not abandoned me'), traditionally linked to Seville's loyalty to King Alfonso X. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/NO8DO))
- A first fountain was built here in 1539 and later given a Renaissance statue of Mercury; that fountain disappeared in a riot in 1712. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_San_Francisco_%28Sevilla%29))
- Today's fountain was made in 1974 by Rafael Manzano Martos and carries the 18th-century Mercury statue. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_San_Francisco_%28Sevilla%29))
- *Note:* Look for NO8DO on the city's street furniture and buildings as a bonus. The Plus Ultra motto of Charles V is reported among the City Hall reliefs but I did not pin down which façade, so it is not used for the question. Processions pass through here in Holy Week (stands are put up).

**4. Plaza del Triunfo** · Plaza del Triunfo, Sevilla (between the Cathedral, the Alcázar and the Archivo de Indias) · 37.38503333, -5.99236667

- The Immaculate Conception monument (sculptor Lorenzo Coullaut Valera, 1918) includes statues of Murillo, Miguel Cid, Martínez Montañés and Juan de Pineda. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Triunfo_%28Sevilla%29))
- The square is named after a Baroque shrine, the Triunfo de Nuestra Señora del Patrocinio, which commemorates the Lisbon earthquake of 1755. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Triunfo_%28Sevilla%29))
- Anecdote: the earthquake struck on 1 November 1755 at about 10:05, during Mass in the cathedral; the Mass was finished out here, and the city's survival was seen as a miracle of the Virgin. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Triunfo_%28Sevilla%29))
- The Archivo de Indias was created in 1785 by Carlos III to gather the papers of Spain's overseas empire; it holds about 43,000 bundles and 8,000 maps and drawings. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archivo_General_de_Indias))
- The Cathedral, the Alcázar and the Archivo de Indias were declared UNESCO World Heritage together in 1987. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Triunfo_%28Sevilla%29))
- *Note:* Sources disagree on the shrine's date (1756 vs 1757) and on the number of earthquake deaths in Seville (6 vs 9), so these are left out of the facts. Alcázar queues form here. All answerable outdoors.

**5. The Giralda, from Plaza Virgen de los Reyes** · Plaza Virgen de los Reyes, Sevilla (north-east side of the Cathedral) · 37.38614, -5.99238

- The figure, called the Giraldillo, was made in 1568 by Bartolomé Morel; it is 3.47 m tall and its body alone weighs 1.2 tonnes. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giraldillo))
- It represents Faith and works as a weathervane: its turning (girar) gave the name 'Giralda' first to the figure and then to the whole tower. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giraldillo))
- Morel signed the contract with the cathedral chapter on 27 August 1566. ([source](https://repositorio.iaph.es/bitstream/11532/332787/3/IAPH_63_1998_PRO_025_TD99_FICHA_TCA_GIRALDILLO_SE.pdf))
- Anecdote: the only fairly exact copy of the Giraldillo crowns the 'Giralda' tower in Kansas City. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giraldillo))
- *Note:* Coordinates are those of the Giralda itself (Wikipedia); the viewing spot is the plaza beside it. A big crowd zone. Sources differ on which hand holds the palm, so the question avoids left/right. Order of stops 4 and 5 is chosen so the leg from Plaza del Triunfo is ~150 m+ around the cathedral's east end; check on the ground.

**6. Plaza de Santa Cruz** · Plaza de Santa Cruz, Barrio de Santa Cruz, Sevilla · 37.38521389, -5.98844167

- The iron cross was made in 1692 by Sebastián Conde from Almonte and is considered the masterpiece of 17th-century Sevillian ironwork. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Santa_Cruz_%28Sevilla%29))
- It first stood at the corner of Calle Sierpes and Calle Rioja, so its correct name is 'Cruz de la Sierpes'; it arrived here in 1921. Look for the four serpents on its base. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Santa_Cruz_%28Sevilla%29))
- The painter Bartolomé Esteban Murillo was buried in 1682 in the church of Santa Cruz that stood on this spot; the church was demolished during the French occupation (1810). A plaque from 1858 recalls him. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Santa_Cruz_%28Sevilla%29))
- The square's present layout was designed by Juan Talavera y Heredia in 1918, ahead of the 1929 Exposition. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Santa_Cruz_%28Sevilla%29))
- *Note:* Narrow lanes, very crowded with tour groups mid-morning. The leg from the Giralda runs through the Santa Cruz lanes (Mateos Gago or Judería); pick the exact path when walking it.

**7. Columbus Monument, Jardines de Murillo** · Paseo de Catalina de Ribera, Sevilla (Jardines de Murillo) · 37.38345278, -5.98791111

- Architecture by Juan Talavera y Heredia, sculpture by Lorenzo Coullaut Valera; paid for by a public subscription launched in 1917 by José Laguillo, editor of the newspaper El Liberal; set up in 1921. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monumento_a_Col%C3%B3n_%28Sevilla%29))
- Between the two stone columns are two bronze caravel prows, with cartouches bearing the names Isabel and Fernando; the monument is 23 m tall and stands in a round fountain basin. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monumento_a_Col%C3%B3n_%28Sevilla%29))
- King Alfonso XIII gave the land to the city; in 1911 stretches of old wall were pulled down to create the gardens, and the same José Laguillo proposed naming them after Murillo. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jardines_de_Murillo))
- The Jardines de Murillo and the Paseo de Catalina de Ribera were declared a Bien de Interés Cultural. ([source](https://www.juntadeandalucia.es/consejo/670302.htm))
- *Note:* Shady, good rest stop. The lion holds an orb (Wikipedia). The longest leg (~0.7 km straight, more on foot) follows, to Plaza de España via the Prado de San Sebastián or the Avenida de Isabel la Católica; check the walking line.

**8. Plaza de España** · Plaza de España, Parque de María Luisa, Sevilla · 37.3770804, -5.98690073

- Designed by architect Aníbal González, it is the largest construction of the 1929 Ibero-American Exposition. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Espa%C3%B1a_%28Sevilla%29))
- The semicircular square has a canal crossed by bridges, a central fountain, tiled benches dedicated to Spanish provinces, medallions of famous Spaniards and coats of arms. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Espa%C3%B1a_%28Sevilla%29))
- Anecdote: Seville itself has no provincial bench; it appears in other murals on the square. ([source](https://www.eldebate.com/espana/andalucia/sevilla/20251031/estas-son-provincias-no-estan-representadas-famosos-azulejos-plaza-espana-sevilla_350182.html))
- It was declared a Bien de Interés Cultural in July 2023; one gallery holds the entrance to the Museo Histórico Militar. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Espa%C3%B1a_%28Sevilla%29))
- *Note:* Free, open-air. Sources disagree on the number of provincial benches (48 vs 46) and on when building began (1914 vs 1924), so these are left out of the facts. Very hot in summer, little shade on the square itself. A good finish: the Parque de María Luisa is next door.

</details>

### The Triana Clay Route · local

*Potters, sailors and the Inquisition across the river.*

Puente de Triana (city side) → Torre del Oro · 8 stops · ≈ 1.9 km · 2.0 h · **needs a fix**

**Why:** Triana has its own story (pottery, sailors, flamenco, the Inquisition castle) and many visitors only cross the bridge for a drink.

**Already offered:** Not verified (search budget ran out). Triana walking tours are common in Seville; check before launch.

**To fix:** short (~1.9 km); a 81 m leg (geofences may overlap); 2 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Puente de Triana (Isabel II bridge) | Look down at the river. How many pillars stand in the water holding up the bridge? | 2 | sourced |
| 2 | Capilla del Carmen, Plaza del Altozano | On top of the chapel's dome, two saints hold a model of which famous tower? | The Giralda | sourced |
| 3 | Iglesia de Nuestra Señora de la O | Look up at the blue-and-white tiled spire of the tower. What sits on the very top? | A crown and an iron cross | sourced |
| 4 | Centro Cerámica Triana (former Cerámica Santa Ana factory) | The old tile factory here took the name of Triana's patron saint. Which saint? | Santa Ana | check |
| 5 | Casa de los Mensaque, Calle San Jacinto | This 1900 house, built for a family of ceramics manufacturers, is used today as what? | Triana district council offices | likely |
| 6 | Capilla de los Marineros (Esperanza de Triana) | The statue in the niche above the entrance is made of what? | Fired clay (terracotta) | sourced |
| 7 | Iglesia de Santa Ana, the 'Cathedral of Triana' | Above the pointed stone side doorway, which coat of arms is carved? | The arms of Castile (a castle) | sourced |
| 8 | Torre del Oro (from the river) | What colour are the tiles on the little dome at the very top of the tower? | Gold | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Puente de Triana (Isabel II bridge)** · Puente de Isabel II, at the Paseo de Cristóbal Colón end (city side), Sevilla · 37.386278, -6.002431

- Officially the Puente de Isabel II, it is the oldest iron bridge preserved in Spain; it was finished in 1852 under Queen Isabel II. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_Isabel_II))
- It replaced a pontoon bridge of boats chained together, first built in 1171 under the Almohad caliph Abu Yacub Yusuf. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_Isabel_II))
- French engineers Fernando Bernadet and Gustavo Steinacher built it; in 1844 the city chose their design with iron arches and two pillars in the river. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_Isabel_II))
- Anecdote: at the opening on 23 February 1852 the archbishop blessed the bridge in its middle, and the day ended with greasy-pole climbing, swimming contests and band concerts. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_Isabel_II))
- Since a 1970s rebuild, a new deck carries the load; the famous iron arches and rings are now decorative. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_Isabel_II))
- *Note:* Stop coordinates left null on purpose: the Wikipedia point for the bridge sits near the Triana end, only ~90 m from stop 2, so the stop should be the city-side end. Start ~400 m from Plaza Nueva (metro/tram); check. Pedestrian pavements on both sides.

**2. Capilla del Carmen, Plaza del Altozano** · Plaza del Altozano, Triana, Sevilla (west end of the Puente de Triana) · 37.38572329, -6.00303027

- The little chapel was designed by Aníbal González and finished in 1928, in exposed brick and Triana ceramics by Emilio García. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capilla_del_Carmen_%28Sevilla%29))
- The two saints are Justa and Rufina, shown protecting the Giralda, inside a small temple with green columns above the dome. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capilla_del_Carmen_%28Sevilla%29))
- Anecdote: Justa and Rufina, Seville's Roman-era patron saints, were potters by trade, fitting for a pottery quarter. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triana_%28Sevilla%29))
- The old chapel was demolished when the bridge got a wider deck (1918); the Virgen del Carmen painting went to Santa Ana and came back here in 1928. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capilla_del_Carmen_%28Sevilla%29))
- In the same square: a statue of bullfighter Juan Belmonte by Venancio Blanco (1972) and a flamenco monument by Jesús Gavira (1994). ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_del_Altozano_%28Sevilla%29))
- *Note:* On the way to stop 3 you pass the Mercado de Triana, built over the Castillo de San Jorge, the Inquisition's seat from 1481 (interpretation centre, opened 2009), and the 35 m Callejón de la Inquisición, whose gate has three ceramic pinnacles and is shut at night. Both are too close to stops 2 and 3 to be stops themselves. Sources: https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castillo_de_San_Jorge_%28Sevilla%29 , https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Callej%C3%B3n_de_la_Inquisici%C3%B3n_%28Sevilla%29

**3. Iglesia de Nuestra Señora de la O** · Calle Castilla, Triana, Sevilla (street number to verify) · 37.38709364, -6.00519598

- The church was built from 1697 and opened in February 1702, to a design by Pedro Romero, built by his sons Pedro and Félix. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_la_O_%28Sevilla%29))
- The square tower was finished in 1699; as one of its tiles says, it was restored in 1756 after the 1755 Lisbon earthquake, when it got the bulb-shaped spire of white and blue tiles. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_la_O_%28Sevilla%29))
- 'La O' refers to the Expectation of Our Lady, an old title for the pregnant Virgin waiting for the birth. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_la_O_%28Sevilla%29))
- It began as a hermitage-hospital dedicated to Saint Brigid of Ireland. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_la_O_%28Sevilla%29))
- *Note:* Bonus question idea: find the tile on the tower that records the 1756 restoration (not verified on the spot). Calle Castilla has narrow pavements and some traffic.

**4. Centro Cerámica Triana (former Cerámica Santa Ana factory)** · Calle San Jorge / Calle Callao corner area, Triana, Sevilla (exact entrance to verify) · 37.38565556, -6.00461389

- In 1870 the potter Antonio Gómez bought kilns here; from 1939 the factory was run by the Rodríguez Díaz brothers as 'Cerámica Santa Ana', after Triana's patron saint. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Cer%C3%A1mica_Triana))
- The site had seven kilns that worked until the late 20th century. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Cer%C3%A1mica_Triana))
- The city opened it as the Centro Cerámica Triana museum in 2014. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Cer%C3%A1mica_Triana))
- At the start of the 20th century Triana had 24 factories making tiles and pottery, thanks to its clay soils. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triana_%28Sevilla%29))
- *Note:* The answer is sourced, but I could not confirm that the name or the saint is visible on the outside, so it is marked 'check'. The museum is ticketed, so answer from the street. If nothing is visible, swap in a façade-tile question during the walk-through. Ceramic shops along Calle San Jorge / Alfarería sell tiles; their names change, so don't use them.

**5. Casa de los Mensaque, Calle San Jacinto** · Calle San Jacinto 33, Triana, Sevilla · 37.384062, -6.004491

- The house at no. 33 dates from 1900, by architect Juan Talavera y de la Vega, built for the Mensaque family, ceramics industrialists. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_San_Jacinto_%28Sevilla%29))
- A bank bought it later; in the 1980s it passed to the City Council, which made it the seat of the Junta Municipal del Distrito Triana. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_San_Jacinto_%28Sevilla%29))
- Calle San Jacinto is about 650 m long and was the old Camino Real (royal road) from Seville to the Aljarafe. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_San_Jacinto_%28Sevilla%29))
- Anecdote: during the Second Republic the street was named after Karl Marx; the name San Jacinto came back in 1936. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_San_Jacinto_%28Sevilla%29))
- At nos. 22 and 24, two other houses designed by José Espiau for the Mensaque ceramic business show rich ceramic decoration. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_San_Jacinto_%28Sevilla%29))
- *Note:* No coordinates found in a source. The district-office use is sourced; marked 'likely' because I have not confirmed a sign is visible. Pedestrian stretch from Altozano to Pagés del Corro. The walk from stop 4 can go via Calle Alfarería and Pagés del Corro to avoid retracing Calle Castilla; check.

**6. Capilla de los Marineros (Esperanza de Triana)** · Calle Pureza, Triana, Sevilla (no. to verify) · 37.384, -6.00102

- The Esperanza brotherhood leased the plot in 1758 and finished the chapel in 1815. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capilla_de_los_Marineros))
- Anecdote: seized by the State after 1868, it was bought in 1873 by two Englishmen and became an Anglican church, then a theatre, cabaret, cinema and cork and coal store. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capilla_de_los_Marineros))
- In 1940 its owner sold it back to the Hermandad de la Esperanza de Triana for a symbolic sum; it was blessed again in 1962. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capilla_de_los_Marineros))
- The niche holds an Immaculate Conception in fired clay by Antonio Illanes (1962); a 1940s tile panel of the Esperanza de Triana was put on the façade in December 2012. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capilla_de_los_Marineros))
- *Note:* Wikipedia gives only rough coordinates (37.384, -6.00102), so the leg to Santa Ana is approximate; it may be just under 150 m, so check on the ground. Packed in Holy Week (Madrugá).

**7. Iglesia de Santa Ana, the 'Cathedral of Triana'** · Calle Pureza, Triana, Sevilla · 37.38283052, -6.00048492

- Begun around 1279-1280 on the orders of King Alfonso X, it replaced the first parish (a hermitage inside the San Jorge castle) and is nicknamed the 'catedral de Triana'. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Santa_Ana_%28Sevilla%29))
- Legend says Alfonso X founded it in 1266 to thank Saint Anne for curing an eye illness, but in 1266 the king was neither in Seville nor ill. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Santa_Ana_%28Sevilla%29))
- Built outside the city walls, it was a fortress-church with battlements and few, small windows. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Santa_Ana_%28Sevilla%29))
- The tower's spire, added from 1629, is covered in white and blue glazed ceramics; the 1755 Lisbon earthquake badly damaged the church. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Santa_Ana_%28Sevilla%29))
- *Note:* The stone side portal is the one on the Gospel (left) nave, the best-preserved of the three doors. Popular 'Velá de Santiago y Santa Ana' fair on nearby Calle Betis in late July (crowds, stalls). Source: https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_Betis

**8. Torre del Oro (from the river)** · Paseo de Cristóbal Colón, El Arenal, Sevilla (riverside) · 37.38245515, -5.99629608

- Almohad tower built between 1220 and 1221; the second body is Mudéjar and the third was added in 1760. It is 36.75 m tall. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_del_Oro))
- The 2005 restoration linked the name 'Tower of Gold' to the golden look of its lime-mortar and pressed-straw coating. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_del_Oro))
- Myth-buster: the gold from the Americas was never stored here; it was kept in the Casa de la Contratación and minted at the Casa de la Moneda. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_del_Oro))
- Since 1944 it houses a Maritime Museum run by the Spanish Navy. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_del_Oro))
- *Note:* Walk south along Calle Betis (views of the Giralda, Torre del Oro and Maestranza) and cross the Puente de San Telmo. The interior museum is ticketed; answer outside. Ends near the Puerta de Jerez metro (check). The final leg is ~0.9 km on foot.

</details>

## Toledo

**Visitors:** Not found. The session's web-search budget ran out before visitor statistics for Toledo could be researched; to be filled from INE's hotel occupancy survey or the city tourism office. Context: the historic city has been a UNESCO World Heritage Site since 1986. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toledo))

**Official walking routes:** Not verified

**Notes:** Toledo sits on a steep rocky hill in a bend of the Tagus. The classic route climbs from the Bisagra gate to Zocodover and then runs downhill to the river, so walk it in that direction. Expect cobbles, steep lanes and steps; it is hard for pushchairs. Summers are very hot (July-August), so start early. Corpus Christi (May/June) is a Fiesta of International Tourist Interest: the procession covers about 2 km of awning-covered streets and changes access around the cathedral (es.wikipedia Toledo). Day-trip crowds from Madrid peak around midday at Zocodover, the cathedral and Santo Tomé. The local route is flat, in the Vega Baja below the walls.

### The Three Cultures Route · classic

*Through the emperor's gate, past a 999 mosque, up to the Alcázar and down to the river bridge.*

Puerta Nueva de Bisagra → Puente de San Martín · 8 stops · ≈ 2.5 km · 2.5 h · **ready to write**

**Why:** It crosses the whole walled city in one line, north gate to west bridge, through Christian, Muslim and Jewish Toledo, with every question answerable from the street.

**Already offered:** Almost certainly covered by many guided and free tours, but not verified: the web search budget ran out before competitors for Toledo could be checked.

**To fix:** 1 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Puerta Nueva de Bisagra | Walk through the gate into the courtyard between its two fronts. Whose statue stands there? | Emperor Charles V | sourced |
| 2 | Mezquita del Cristo de la Luz | The mosque's building inscription on its façade is unique in the Islamic West because it is made entirely of pieces of one material. Which? | Brick | sourced |
| 3 | Plaza de Zocodover | Which arch leads out of the square on its eastern side, towards the river? | Arco de la Sangre | likely |
| 4 | Alcázar de Toledo (exterior) | The Alcázar is a huge square block. How many corner towers does it have? | Four | sourced |
| 5 | Catedral Primada (west façade, Plaza del Ayuntamiento) | Stand in Plaza del Ayuntamiento. Between the bell tower and the unfinished 'second tower', how many great doorways are there? | Three | sourced |
| 6 | Iglesia de Santo Tomé (tower) | The Mudéjar bell tower was once a minaret. What is set into its brickwork as decoration? | Glazed ceramic pieces | sourced |
| 7 | Monasterio de San Juan de los Reyes (exterior) | What hangs on the monastery's outer walls, a reminder of Christian captives set free? | Iron chains | sourced |
| 8 | Puente de San Martín | On the inner wall of the entrance tower, an imperial coat of arms has seated kings on each side. How many kings? | Two | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Puerta Nueva de Bisagra** · Puerta de Bisagra, Calle Real del Arrabal / Paseo de Merchán, 45003 Toledo · 39.8625, -4.0252

- It is called 'Nueva' (new) because a smaller, older gate nearby is known as the Puerta Antigua de Bisagra, or Puerta de Alfonso VI. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_Nueva_de_Bisagra))
- Its Muslim name was 'bab a Ssaqra'. It was rebuilt from about 1540 to 1576, with Alonso de Covarrubias among the masters. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_Nueva_de_Bisagra))
- It has two separate fronts joined by battlemented walls, with a courtyard between them. A statue of Charles V stands in the courtyard. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_Nueva_de_Bisagra))
- A 1999 excavation found that the Renaissance gate stands on an older bent entrance from before Alfonso VI took the city in 1085. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_Nueva_de_Bisagra))
- *Note:* Traffic passes through the gate, so take care with children. Charles V's double-headed eagle shield on the outer front is a well-known alternative question (not sourced here). The route climbs from here.

**2. Mezquita del Cristo de la Luz** · Calle Cristo de la Luz 22, 45001 Toledo (reached via the Puerta del Sol) · 39.86055556, -4.02425

- Of the ten mosques Toledo once had, this is the best preserved. It was built in 999, as its façade inscription says. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cristo_de_la_Luz))
- The inscription, found in 1889, names Ahmad ibn Hadidi as the patron and Musa ibn Ali as the architect, and dates it to December 999. It is made solely of fragments of ordinary bricks. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cristo_de_la_Luz))
- After the 1085 Christian conquest, a Romanesque-Mudéjar apse was added around 1187, one of the oldest examples of that style. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cristo_de_la_Luz))
- The outside is red brick and masonry, and the inside has small domes that cover the prayer hall. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cristo_de_la_Luz))
- *Note:* The interior needs a ticket. Check that the brick inscription can be seen from the street (it is on the south-west façade). The Puerta del Sol gate is about 100 m before this stop, too close for its own stop. Its name comes from a relief with a sun on the outer side (source: es.wikipedia Puerta del Sol (Toledo)), so it can be used as a bonus. Steep climb.

**3. Plaza de Zocodover** · Plaza de Zocodover, 45001 Toledo · 39.85945556, -4.021375

- The name comes from the Arabic 'sūq ad-dawābb', meaning 'market of beasts of burden'. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Zocodover))
- Bulls were run here, and the Inquisition held autos-da-fé and public executions in the square. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Zocodover))
- After a fire destroyed the old square on 29 Oct 1589, a new one was built; Juan de Herrera designed part of it under Philip II. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Zocodover))
- In 1502 Isabella I approved a plan to widen it all the way to the Alcázar, but the plan was never carried out. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Zocodover))
- *Note:* The Wikipedia article shows the Arco de la Sangre, but its exact side of the square is not sourced, so check it. Busiest square in town. There are toilets and the tourist train nearby.

**4. Alcázar de Toledo (exterior)** · Calle Unión s/n / Cuesta de Carlos V, 45001 Toledo · 39.85805556, -4.02055556

- It is a square building, 60 m on each side, with four large towers, each topped by a Madrid-style slate spire. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alc%C3%A1zar_de_Toledo))
- Its name comes from the Arabic 'al-qasr', meaning fortress. It was a Roman palace in the 3rd century. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alc%C3%A1zar_de_Toledo))
- Charles I (Emperor Charles V) and his son Philip II restored it in the 1540s. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alc%C3%A1zar_de_Toledo))
- In July 1528 Charles I received Hernán Cortés here after the conquest of the Aztec Empire. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alc%C3%A1zar_de_Toledo))
- *Note:* The interior is the Army Museum and needs a ticket; this question is exterior only. Zocodover to the Alcázar is only about 170 m, so the meeting point could be set on the south side to lengthen the leg.

**5. Catedral Primada (west façade, Plaza del Ayuntamiento)** · Plaza del Ayuntamiento, 45002 Toledo · 39.85708333, -4.02388889

- Building began in 1226 under Ferdinand III. The last Gothic vaults were closed in 1493, under the Catholic Monarchs. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_de_Toledo))
- It is built of white stone from Olihuelas (Olías del Rey), and is popularly called 'Dives Toletana', 'the rich one of Toledo'. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_de_Toledo))
- From left to right, the west front has the bell tower, the Puerta del Infierno, the Puerta del Perdón, the Puerta del Juicio Final, and an unfinished 'second tower' used as a chapel. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_de_Toledo))
- The Puerta del Perdón, begun in 1418 by Alvar Martínez, got its name from the indulgences granted to penitents. Today it opens only on great occasions, such as a new archbishop taking office. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_de_Toledo))
- Hidden in the Puerta del Reloj arch is a carved scene that may show the legend of Charlemagne and the Moorish princess Galiana. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catedral_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_de_Toledo))
- *Note:* The interior needs a ticket. There is a nice echo with Málaga's one-tower 'Manquita' if the cities are cross-promoted. Corpus Christi stands and awnings fill this area in late spring.

**6. Iglesia de Santo Tomé (tower)** · Plaza del Conde 4 / Calle Santo Tomé, 45002 Toledo · 39.85682222, -4.02813333

- The church is first mentioned in the 12th century, built on the site of an 11th-century mosque. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Santo_Tom%C3%A9_(Toledo)))
- Gonzalo Ruiz de Toledo, lord of Orgaz, rebuilt it in the early 14th century and turned the minaret into a Mudéjar bell tower of masonry and brick with glazed ceramic inlays. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Santo_Tom%C3%A9_(Toledo)))
- Legend: when the lord of Orgaz (died 1323) was buried, Saint Stephen and Saint Augustine appeared to lay him in his tomb. El Greco painted the scene in 'The Burial of the Count of Orgaz'. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Santo_Tom%C3%A9_(Toledo)))
- The painting still hangs here, entered from the back of the church. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_de_Santo_Tom%C3%A9_(Toledo)))
- *Note:* Seeing the painting needs a ticket, and there are queues at midday. The question is about the tower. The leg to San Juan de los Reyes passes the Tránsito synagogue and Santa María la Blanca (both ticketed). Those could be bonus mentions.

**7. Monasterio de San Juan de los Reyes (exterior)** · Calle Reyes Católicos 17, 45002 Toledo · 39.85769444, -4.03158333

- Isabella I commissioned it to commemorate the victory of Toro (1476) and the birth of Prince Juan (1478), and as a planned royal mausoleum. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasterio_de_San_Juan_de_los_Reyes))
- The architect was Juan Guas, but his name was only found in 1853, on an inscription in the church of San Justo y Pastor. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasterio_de_San_Juan_de_los_Reyes))
- The chains on the outside are those of Christian captives freed when Ferdinand the Catholic reconquered Málaga and Baeza. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasterio_de_San_Juan_de_los_Reyes))
- The church was finished in 1495. Inside, the yoke-and-arrows emblem and the motto 'tanto monta' repeat everywhere. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasterio_de_San_Juan_de_los_Reyes))
- *Note:* A great cross-city link to the Málaga route (the chains come from Málaga's 1487 conquest). The interior and cloister need a ticket.

**8. Puente de San Martín** · Puente de San Martín, Bajada de San Martín, 45002 Toledo · 39.8566, -4.034226

- This medieval bridge over the Tagus is named after the parish it belonged to, San Martín. It probably took the Alcántara bridge as its model, but needed more arches because the river is wider here. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_San_Mart%C3%ADn_(Toledo)))
- Around 1355 Peter I of Castile reportedly set fire to the bridge's gates, and it was damaged again in 1368. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_San_Mart%C3%ADn_(Toledo)))
- Archbishop Pedro Tenorio restored it around 1390, building the great central arch and the two battlemented towers at the ends. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_San_Mart%C3%ADn_(Toledo)))
- An inscription with the imperial shield flanked by two seated kings recalls the bridge's later widening (under Charles II) and paving. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_San_Mart%C3%ADn_(Toledo)))
- It was declared a national monument on 21 Dec 1921. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_San_Mart%C3%ADn_(Toledo)))
- *Note:* There is a well-known legend that the builder's wife set fire to the scaffolding. It was not sourced, so it is left out. A zipline crosses the river near the bridge (seasonal). Steep descent from San Juan de los Reyes. Getting back up means a city bus or climbing again.

</details>

### The Vega Baja Route · local

*Below the walls: a cardinal's hospital, a Roman racetrack, a saint's hermitage and the sword factory.*

Hospital de Tavera → Antigua Fábrica de Armas (UCLM campus) · 6 stops · ≈ 3.2 km · 1.75 h · **ready to write**

**Why:** Day-trippers rarely leave the hilltop, but the flat Vega Baja below the walls holds Roman, Visigothic and industrial Toledo, where locals jog, picnic and study.

**Already offered:** Not verified: the web search budget ran out before checking for Vega Baja walking tours.

**To fix:** 1 question(s) to confirm on site

| # | Stop | On-site question | Answer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Hospital de Tavera (exterior) | Look at the façade. The ground-floor windows are rectangular. What shape are most of the windows on the upper floor? | Semicircular (arched) | sourced |
| 2 | Puerta de Alfonso VI (Puerta Antigua de Bisagra) | This old gate was kept shut for centuries and only reopened after a restoration. In which century did it reopen? | 20th century | check |
| 3 | Circo Romano | After the Romans, people used this racetrack for something else, and you can still see traces of it. What? | A cemetery | sourced |
| 4 | Puerta del Cambrón | A statue of Toledo's patron saint was carved for this gate. Which saint? | Santa Leocadia | sourced |
| 5 | Ermita del Cristo de la Vega | What monument stands in front of the hermitage? | A monument to the Sacred Heart of Jesus | sourced |
| 6 | Antigua Fábrica de Armas (UCLM campus) | Inside the old factory grounds, one tower has a road running underneath it. What is on the tower? | A clock | sourced |

<details><summary>Stop details, facts and sources</summary>

**1. Hospital de Tavera (exterior)** · Calle Duque de Lerma 2 / Paseo de Madrid, 45003 Toledo · 39.865042, -4.024421

- Cardinal Tavera ordered it built between 1541 and 1603. Alonso de Covarrubias started the work and Bartolomé Bustamante finished it. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_de_Tavera))
- It was nicknamed 'el hospital de afuera' (the hospital outside) because it lay far from the old town; there was already a Hospital de Santa Cruz inside the walls. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_de_Tavera))
- It looks like a Florentine Renaissance palace with an Italian rusticated façade. The upper windows are semicircular, with the arrangement reversed at the ends. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_de_Tavera))
- Inside are Cardinal Tavera's white marble tomb by Alonso Berruguete and an altarpiece designed by El Greco and built by his son Jorge Manuel. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_de_Tavera))
- It still belongs to the House of Medinaceli and holds the Museo Fundación Lerma and the Archivo Histórico de la Nobleza (270 noble family archives). ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_de_Tavera))
- *Note:* Museum ticketed; the exterior is free. The nearest public transport is not sourced, so check bus lines and the bus station distance.

**2. Puerta de Alfonso VI (Puerta Antigua de Bisagra)** · Calle Carretas / Paseo de Recaredo, by the Puerta Nueva de Bisagra, 45003 Toledo · 39.8622, -4.0262

- Its official name is Puerta de Alfonso VI, but for centuries it was called Bisagra Vieja, wrongly identified with the medieval Bisagra gate. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_Antigua_de_Bisagra))
- Its original name is unknown; it may have been the 'Puerta Almaguera', from Bab al-Maqbar ('Cemetery Gate'). ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_Antigua_de_Bisagra))
- Under Muslim rule it was one of the entrances from the Vega. After the new Bisagra gate was built, it stayed closed except for special occasions, until it was restored and reopened in 1905. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_Antigua_de_Bisagra))
- *Note:* The answer can't be seen unless a plaque gives the date, so look for one or rewrite the question on site (e.g. arch shape). The gate is only about 120 m from the classic route's start, so the two routes can share a meeting area.

**3. Circo Romano** · Parque del Circo Romano, Avenida de Carlos III, 45004 Toledo · 39.86426389, -4.02920833

- The circus was built in the 1st century AD, under Augustus or Tiberius, on the north side of the Roman city. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circo_romano_de_Toledo))
- It measured 422 m by about 112 m and held an estimated 15,000-30,000 spectators. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circo_romano_de_Toledo))
- More than half of it is still unexcavated. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circo_romano_de_Toledo))
- In Muslim times traders set up shop on the stands, and later the circus became a cemetery whose remains are visible to the naked eye. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circo_romano_de_Toledo))
- The remains were consolidated and the park landscaped in 2011-2012. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circo_romano_de_Toledo))
- *Note:* Free public park, with a playground. Check what traces of the cemetery can be seen and whether there is a panel.

**4. Puerta del Cambrón** · Puerta del Cambrón, Paseo de la Rosa / Calle Cambrón, 45002 Toledo · 39.85933889, -4.03225

- It was also called the Puerta de los Judíos (Jews' Gate) and the Puerta de Santa Leocadia. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_del_Cambr%C3%B3n))
- Its name may come from a thorny bush ('cambrón') that grew on top of a ruined tower before it was rebuilt in 1576. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_del_Cambr%C3%B3n))
- The Renaissance gate has two pairs of towers and two arches, in stone and brick. Juan Bautista Monegro is thought to have carved its Santa Leocadia figure. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_del_Cambr%C3%B3n))
- On 23 Aug 1936, militiamen shot about eighty people here, many of them priests and Catholic laypeople. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_del_Cambr%C3%B3n))
- *Note:* The 1936 fact is sensitive for school groups; the lesson editor may prefer to leave it out. Check that the statue is visible and on which face. The leg from the Circo Romano is about 600 m.

**5. Ermita del Cristo de la Vega** · Paseo del Cristo de la Vega s/n, 45004 Toledo · 39.86091667, -4.03428056

- By tradition, Toledo's patron saint Leocadia was buried here; first a Visigothic hermitage and then a basilica were built over the spot. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermita_del_Cristo_de_la_Vega))
- Late-Roman remains found on the site may belong to a martyrium of Leocadia. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermita_del_Cristo_de_la_Vega))
- The building keeps a Mudéjar apse from the earlier church. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermita_del_Cristo_de_la_Vega))
- It was declared a national monument on 3 Jun 1931, under the Second Republic. The Sacred Heart monument in front was finished in 1933. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermita_del_Cristo_de_la_Vega))
- *Note:* José Zorrilla's legend 'A buen juez, mejor testigo' (the Christ who detached his arm to swear) is set here and is a superb lesson anecdote, but no source was found in this session, so it is left out of facts. Add it after sourcing. Opening hours not checked; the question is exterior only.

**6. Antigua Fábrica de Armas (UCLM campus)** · Avenida Carlos III s/n, 45004 Toledo (Puerta de Carlos III / Sabatini) · 39.865991, -4.042913

- Charles III founded a sword factory in Toledo, modelled on Torre Annunziata in Naples. It began in 1761 in the old Mint, on today's Calle Núñez de Arce. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A1brica_de_armas_de_Toledo))
- In 1777 he ordered a new building by architect Francesco Sabatini on the Tagus, at the 'playa de las Barcas', to use water power. Most of it was finished in 1780. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A1brica_de_armas_de_Toledo))
- At first it made three kinds of sword: for cavalry, dragoons and infantry. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A1brica_de_armas_de_Toledo))
- French troops invaded the factory twice, in 1808 and 1823. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A1brica_de_armas_de_Toledo))
- In 1998 Defence sold it to the city, which passed it to the Universidad de Castilla-La Mancha; it is now the science and technology campus. ([source](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A1brica_de_armas_de_Toledo))
- *Note:* It is a university campus: check public access and opening hours, and that groups are welcome (term time and weekends may differ). Entrances are the Puerta de Carlos III (Sabatini) and the Puerta de Obreros. The last leg from Cristo de la Vega is about 950 m along Avenida de Carlos III.

</details>
